Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                                                                                         1
Introduction




Preface

The Public Accounts of Alberta are prepared in accordance with the Financial Administration Act
and the Sustainable Fiscal Planning and Reporting Act. The Public Accounts consist of the annual
report of the Government of Alberta and the annual reports of each ministry.

On October 16, 2024, the government announced new ministry structures. As such, the
responsibilities for Continuing Care, previously held by the Ministry of Health were transferred to
the Ministry of Seniors, Community and Social Services. The 2024-25 Annual Report reflects the
2024-27 Ministry business plan, the Government of Alberta Strategic Plan, as well as the ministry’s
activities and accomplishments during the 2024-25 fiscal year, which ended on March 31, 2025.

The Annual Report of the Government of Alberta contains Budget 2024 Key Results, the audited
Consolidated Financial Statements and Performance Results, which is a comparison of actual
performance results to desired results set out in the government’s strategic plan.

This annual report of the Ministry of Health contains the Minister’s Accountability Statement, the
ministry’s Financial Information and Results Analysis, which is a comparison of actual performance
results to desired results set out in the Ministry Business Plan. This ministry annual report also
includes:

    •          the financial statements of entities making up the ministry including Alberta Health
               Services (AHS) and the Health Quality Council of Alberta (HQCA) for which the minister
               is responsible; and

    •          other financial information as required by the Financial Administration Act and
               Sustainable Fiscal Planning and Reporting Act, as separate reports, to the extent that the
               ministry has anything to report.

All Ministry annual reports should be considered along with the Government of Alberta Annual
Report to provide a complete overview of government’s commitment to openness, accountability,
and fiscal transparency.

Ministers’ Accountability Statement

The ministry’s annual report for the year ended March 31, 2025, was prepared under my direction
in accordance with the Sustainable Fiscal Planning and Reporting Act and the government’s
accounting policies. All the government’s policy decisions as at June 3, 2025, with material
economic or fiscal implications of which I am aware have been considered in the preparation of this
report.

Introduction




Message from the Ministers

                                 Since our refocusing initiative was first introduced, we have made
                                 tremendous progress on implementing a reliable, accountable
                                 health care system that puts patients first while empowering health
                                 care workers.

                                 We know how important it is to get this work right. That’s why
                                 we’ve focused on engaging with the people who matter most:
                                 Albertans. During our first round of public engagement, we heard
                                 from almost 30,000 people through in-person and virtual town
                                 halls and using online tools. We launched a second round of
                                 engagement in January 2025 to share information and receive
                                 additional feedback, which has helped shape our path forward.

                                 Our 2024-25 annual report reflects the progress that has been
                                 made to date, including the introduction of four provincial health
                                 agencies that are responsible for overseeing key parts of the health
                                 care system: Primary Care Alberta, Acute Care Alberta, Assisted
                                 Living Alberta and Recovery Alberta. Each of these provincial
                                 health agencies reports to a minister, improving accountability and
                                 access to services across the province.

In addition to our refocusing work, the 2024-25 annual report highlights significant work that was
done over the past year to improve health outcomes for Albertans.

Specific to acute care, we added 83 emergency department physicians and 157 more emergency
department nurses in 2024-25, and we completed a record number of 318,601 surgeries,
surpassing the target of 310,000. We also opened the $1.4 billion Arthur J.E. Child Comprehensive
Cancer Centre, a state-of-the-art facility that offers 160 inpatient beds, chemotherapy chairs, exam
rooms, radiation vaults, outpatient services and clinical trial space to support Albertans living with
cancer.

To support improved access to primary care services, we introduced a new Primary Care Physician
Compensation Model that was developed in collaboration with the Alberta Medical Association.
This model encourages physicians to grow their patient panels while rewarding high-volume, high-
quality care with incentives. We also introduced a Nurse Practitioner Primary Care Program to
support nurse practitioners so they can practise comprehensive patient care autonomously and
operate their own practices, or practise autonomously in existing primary care settings, and we
launched the Rural and Remote Family Medicine Resident Physician Bursary Program to support
family medicine residents who commit to practising in rural or remote areas.

In total, Budget 2024 provided $26.2 billion in operating expenses to prioritize the delivery of high-
quality, reliable health services across the province. Budget 2024 also provided $4.5 billion over
three years to support capital projects like the redevelopment of the Red Deer Regional Hospital
Centre, plans for a stand-alone Stollery Children’s Hospital in Edmonton, and the replacement of the
Beaverlodge Health Centre.

Introduction




Management’s Responsibility for Reporting

The Ministry of Health includes Alberta Health, AHS, Primary Care Alberta, and the Health Quality
Council of Alberta.

The executives of the individual entities within the ministry have the primary responsibility and
accountability for the respective entities. Collectively, the executives ensure the ministry complies
with all relevant legislation, regulations and policies.

Ministry business plans, annual reports, performance results and the supporting management
information are integral to the government’s fiscal and strategic plan, annual report, quarterly
reports and other financial and performance reporting.

Responsibility for the integrity and objectivity of the accompanying ministry financial
information and performance results for the ministry rests with the Minister of Health. Under the
direction of the minister, I oversee the preparation of the ministry’s annual report, which includes
the financial information, performance results on all objectives and initiatives identified in the
Ministry Business Plan, and performance results for all ministry-supported commitments that
were included in the 2024-27 Government of Alberta Strategic Plan. The financial information
and performance results, out of necessity, include amounts that are based on estimates and
judgments. The financial information is prepared using the government’s stated accounting
policies, which are based on Canadian public sector accounting standards. The performance
measures are prepared in accordance with the following criteria:

•   Reliable – information used in applying performance measure methodologies agrees with the
    underlying source data for the current and prior years’ results.
•   Understandable – the performance measure methodologies and results are presented clearly.
•   Comparable – the methodologies for performance measure preparation are applied consistently
    for the current and prior years’ results.
•   Complete – outcomes, performance measures and related targets match those included in the
    ministry’s Budget 2024.

As deputy minister, in addition to program responsibilities, I am responsible for the ministry’s
financial administration and reporting functions. The ministry maintains systems of financial
management and internal control which considers costs, benefits and risks that are designed
to:

•   provide reasonable assurance that transactions are properly authorized, executed in
    accordance with prescribed legislation and regulations, and properly recorded to maintain
    accountability of public money;
•   provide information to manage and report on performance;
•   safeguard the assets and properties of the province under ministry administration;
•   provide Executive Council, the President of Treasury Board and Minister of Finance, and the
    Minister of Health the information needed to fulfil their responsibilities; and
•   facilitate preparation of ministry business plans and annual reports required under the
    Sustainable Fiscal Planning and Reporting Act.

                                                                                       Results Analysis




Ministry Overview
Organizational Structure
The Government of Alberta is refocusing Alberta’s health care system to improve health outcomes
for Albertans and empower health care workers to deliver quality care across the province. A
refocused health care system will provide Albertans with the necessary care when and where they
need it, enhance government’s ability to provide system-wide oversight, set system priorities, and
ensure accountability for those priorities on behalf of Albertans. As part of this refocused approach,
the ministry continues to support Albertans’ health and well-being throughout their lives by
protecting public health and promoting wellness; coordinating and delivering safe, person-
centered, quality health care services; planning capital infrastructure; supporting innovative
information management and technologies; regulating health care; and funding the health system.

In fall 2024, amendments to the Provincial Health Agencies Act provided the Ministry of Health with
a clear mandate to transform Alberta’s health care delivery and governance. This new, outcome-
focused approach replaces the single regional health authority model with a more integrated
system comprised of four provincial health agencies (PHAs), each focused on a key sector of care:
Primary Care Alberta (PCA), operational since February 1, 2025; Acute Care Alberta (ACA),
operational as of April 1, 2025; Assisted Living Alberta (ALA), established as a legal entity on April
1, 2025; and Recovery Alberta (RA), which began operations on September 1, 2024. These agencies
will work in coordination under the ministry's oversight to improve health outcomes and ensure
greater accountability across the system.

The Ministry of Health currently consists of Alberta Health, ACA, PCA, and the HQCA. The Ministry
of Mental Health and Addiction oversees RA, and the Ministry of Seniors, Community and Social
Services oversees ALA. The alignment of PHAs within the health system is depicted in the chart
below. AHS will continue to have a role in the refocused health care system by concentrating on the
delivery of acute care services. To promote continued alignment within the system, the province
established an Integration Council, which all four PHAs will be a part of. The goal of the Integration
Council is to ensure all ministries, PHAs, and service delivery providers work together to deliver
better health outcomes for Albertans.

The Ministry of Health relies on collaboration with a range of health professionals; partners and
organizations in the health and social sectors; academic and research institutions; First Nations,
Metis Settlements, and the Métis Nation of Alberta; and other orders of government to deliver
health services. The Minister of Health is supported by parliamentary secretaries for rural health
and health workforce engagement.




Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                          9
Results Analysis



Alberta Health establishes the Government of Alberta’s strategic direction for health. The
department advises the government on health policy, legislation and standards, and public health
concerns; monitors and reports on health system performance; sets policies and priorities for the
electronic/digital health environment; provides oversight; and ensures accountability across the
health system.

ACA, the PHA for acute care, will oversee the governance and coordination of acute care services
across Alberta by working directly with acute care service providers to reduce wait times for
emergency departments (EDs) and surgeries, lower emergency medical services (EMS) response
times, and enhance the overall care experience. The goal is to deliver timelier, more coordinated
acute care services across the province. Alberta acute care providers include AHS, Covenant Health,
the Lamont Health Care Centre and chartered surgical facilities.

PCA, the PHA responsible for the primary care health services sector, coordinates primary health
care services and contributes to the transparent oversight of the health system at the provincial
level. PCA will focus on ensuring every Albertan is attached to a family physician or a nurse
practitioner (NP), providing timely access to high-quality primary care services, and supporting an
integrated team of health professionals to provide comprehensive primary care with appropriate
access to patient health information.

The HQCA works collaboratively with health system partners on a variety of topics and initiatives
to improve patient safety, person-centered care, and overall health service quality across the
province. The HQCA’s role will expand to support Alberta Health and the PHAs to set performance
standards and performance indicators and to support audit and compliance functions.

The Office of the Alberta Health Advocates reports to the Minister of Health to improve patient care
and the health system. The office supports Albertans in navigating the health system and accessing
the appropriate resolution services to address health care concerns, including those specific to the
Alberta Health Act, seniors, their families, and service providers.

                                                                                        Results Analysis



Operational Overview

Department of Health
With direction from the minister, the deputy minister is responsible for the daily operations of the
Department of Health, which is structured as follows:

Deputy Minister’s Office – provides leadership to the health system to ensure the delivery of
quality health services, drives innovation, and builds and maintains collaborative relationships
across government ministries, AHS, the HQCA, and the PHAs. The office provides policy
coordination and issues management for the minister and leadership in priority setting, decision-
making, and operations, as well as ministry correspondence services.

Acute Care Division – leads the province’s renewed focus on acute care services, including
developing standards, monitoring and evaluating the acute care and Emergency Health Services
(EHS) performance, and working with partner divisions to oversee the delivery of hospital care,
urgent care, cancer care, provincial program organ and tissue donation, and transplantation
services clinical operations, surgeries, and EHS. The division also supports strategic policy for acute
care, working with partners to develop solutions for the acute care and EHS sectors, oversees the
governance and legislative compliance of EHS delivery and is currently supporting the
implementation of the new Alberta EMS Standing Committee (AESC).

Finance and Capital Planning Division – develops and manages the ministry’s budget and
oversees financial activities within the Department of Health. The division provides financial advice,
prepares annual financial statements, and leads corporate planning and reporting. The Finance and
Capital Planning Division (FCP) is responsible for AHS accountability, capital planning and
coordination of infrastructure projects with Alberta Infrastructure and AHS. The division also leads
integrated health system planning by developing system planning policies to support a more
coordinated and outcome-focused provincial health care system. Additionally, FCP manages the
department’s grant approval process, provides corporate administrative and contracting services,
and oversees registration, designation and acute care bed survey processes and reporting.

Health Information Systems Division – provides leadership and strategic direction for the
development and implementation of policy and standards for the provincial eHealth environment,
and the Department of Health. The division is responsible for Alberta Netcare (electronic health
record), Community Information Integration and Central Patient Attachment Registry, Connect
Care (clinical information system), and MyHealth Records (personal health record), including
health information policy and advice, stakeholder engagement, strategic planning and project and
system delivery and operations.

Health Workforce Division – develops and implements health workforce and system policies
related to compensation for insured health care providers and the self-regulation of health
professions. Working in collaboration with stakeholders, the division oversees the implementation
of key health workforce planning and priorities, such as the health workforce strategy, and
provides health workforce planning support, enabling a fiscally sustainable and effective health
workforce to support Albertans’ needs. Together, these initiatives support the government’s
commitment to build a health care system that is sustainable and serves the needs of Albertans.

Indigenous Health Division – leads government efforts to improve the health and well-being of
First Nations, Métis, and Inuit peoples in Alberta through culturally safe health care service
delivery. The division facilitates this by including Indigenous partners’ perspectives and reflecting
patients’ unique health needs across all health program and policy planning. The division leads key
mandate priorities, such as strengthening Alberta Health’s capacity to develop effective health
policy and conduct strategic planning, building respectful and trusting relationships, advancing the


Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                      11
Results Analysis



implementation of the Indigenous Modernizing Alberta’s Primary Health Care System (MAPS)
initiative, and working collaboratively with First Nations, Métis, and Inuit partners, and health
system providers to build a culturally safe health care system.

Pharmaceutical and Supplementary Benefits Division – oversees and provides governance to the
Alberta Health Care Insurance Plan (AHCIP) and all government-sponsored supplementary health
benefits programs (i.e., Coverage for Seniors, Non-Group Coverage, Assured Income for the Severely
Handicapped, Income Support, Alberta Adult/Child Health Benefit programs, Ukrainian Evacuee
Health Benefit, Alberta Aids to Daily Living, and Dental and Optical Assistance for Seniors) that
provide Albertans with pharmaceutical, optical, dental, and other medical supports (wheelchairs,
prosthetics, oxygen, medical/surgical supplies, etc.). The division is also responsible for Alberta’s
participation in the National Blood Program and advising the minister in their role as a corporate
member of Canadian Blood Services.

Primary Care and Rural Health Division – responsible for developing policy and performance
oversight of primary care, including new primary care agency (Primary Care Alberta) and models
(e.g., Regional Primary Health Care Networks) and developing related accountability mechanisms,
such as standards, guidelines, and legislative tools. The division is also responsible for developing
and implementing key policy initiatives aligned with the government’s renewed focus on primary
care, such as the implementation of the MAPS initiative, supporting the administration and
governance of Primary Care Networks (PCNs), and strengthening relationships with key
stakeholders. The division will oversee primary and community health; chronic disease
management; and the administration and governance of PCNs to meet the needs of Albertans. The
division collaborates with stakeholders, such as physicians, NPs, registered nurses, licensed
practical nurses, health professions’ regulatory colleges, and health system leaders from academia
and the community to support the provision of quality primary health care services to Albertans.
The mandate of the division is achieved through evidence-informed and value-oriented initiatives,
promotion of the patient medical home initiative through PCNs and their governance structure,
performance monitoring and evaluation, and collaboration with health professions’ regulatory
colleges to support patient safety. The division is also responsible for the development of rural
health-related policy and is responsible for ensuring that rural and remote perspectives are
considered and incorporated in Alberta Health policies.

Public Health Division – provides strategic direction and leadership on emerging public health
risks, communicable diseases, immunization, compliance monitoring, environmental public health,
newborn screening, health promotion, and emergency preparedness, response and recovery,
through the assessment, development and implementation of provincial policies, regulations,
strategies, and standards. The division carries out these functions to support innovation and
engagement with Albertans towards wellness, health promotion, and injury and disease prevention.
To support health system quality, the division collaborates with partners to perform compliance
and monitoring activities, and enforcement of the acts, regulations and standards administered by
the division in the areas of infection prevention and control oversight, and protection for persons
receiving certain care or support services.

Strategic Policy and Performance Division – responsible for providing leadership for health care
research and innovation, including monitoring, assessing, and improving health system
performance. The division is the primary source for Alberta’s overall health system data standards
and analytics, evaluating the health system’s performance to support evidence-based policy
decisions for all health sectors. The division is also responsible for the Freedom of Information and
Protection of Privacy Act (FOIP), legislative planning, strategic policy coordination, governance of
over 45 health public agencies and over 70 heath foundations (including 450 member
opportunities), and all Cabinet-related materials, as well as intergovernmental relations. The



12                                                                         Health | Annual Report 2024–2025
                                                                                     Results Analysis



division works with other divisions to develop performance and reporting standards for the health
care system.

System Refocusing Division – responsible for implementing the new direction for Alberta's health
care system to improve health outcomes for Albertans and empower health care workers to deliver
quality care across the province. This includes supporting the transition to a unified provincial
health care system by helping establish governance structures and operational processes for the
newly created sector-based PHAs for acute care, continuing care, primary care, and mental health
and addiction. The division also supports ministry divisions, AHS, and system partners in aligning
with the new direction, and engages with health care workers and members of the public to
understand the current system needs and support for a smooth transition. In addition, the division
provides secretariat support to the Integration Council, which is tasked with guiding and
monitoring the implementation of the integrated health system and ensuring coordination across
all PHAs.

Office of the Chief Medical Officer of Health – monitors and reports on the health of Albertans and
advises on actions to protect and promote the health of the public under authority of the Public
Health Act. This includes legislated responsibilities related to disease surveillance, communicable
disease outbreaks, infection prevention and control measures, health risk assessments, and states
of public health emergencies. The Office of the Chief Medical Officer of Health provides strategic
leadership, oversight, support and clinical expertise on issues of public health importance to
Albertans. The office works closely with diverse partners within and beyond the health system,
including the Department of Health’s Public Health Division, Primary Care and Rural Health
Division, Medical Officers of Health at AHS, and Indigenous Services Canada to facilitate policies,
processes and programs to prevent chronic diseases, control the spread of communicable diseases,
support health surveillance, and strengthen the public health system in Alberta.

Communications and Public Engagement [CPE]-Health –provides Albertans and health system
partners with information about ministry policies, programs, and initiatives. The CPE-Health team
works with department staff to develop and implement communication plans and offers
communications support, such as media relations, issues management, writing and editing services,
product development, and online communications services. CPE-Health also works closely with
AHS and other reporting entities to coordinate ministry communications.

Health Law – through Alberta Justice, a team of lawyers support all aspects of the Department of
Health’s activities, ranging from grant agreements and contracting and procurement to developing
and interpreting legislation and general legal advice to the ministry.

Human Resources – through the Public Service Commission, Human Resources is dedicated to
delivering programs, supporting initiatives, and providing human resource expertise and services
that attract, retain, and engage the department’s workforce. The branch works in partnership with
managers and employees to build and sustain workforce capacity to achieve business goals and
create an environment where employees are respected, valued, engaged and resilient.

Procurement and System Optimization Secretariat – ensures procurement frameworks are
developed and maintained to support health care contract management related to services and
commodities, including governance, risk management, performance management, compliance, and
public accountability. The secretariat supports system-wide optimization (i.e., cross-sector
optimization projects, etc.) and leads development of standing offers for system-wide ancillary
services to support system-wide optimization, where appropriate. The secretariat establishes
spending and urgency thresholds to support local procurement and decision-making. The overall
goal is to provide a system-wide procurement oversight function to ensure efficient, open, and
transparent procurement processes are maintained for safeguarding public funds, supporting

Results Analysis



Alberta's health service delivery partners, while adapting to local needs where required and where
it makes sense

Key Highlights
The Ministry of Health focused on accomplishing [number of outcomes] identified in the 2023-26
Ministry Business Plan:

•    Enhanced timely access to an effective, safe and responsive publicly funded health care
     system that provides Albertans with the necessary care when and where they need it.
• A modernized and refocused health care system that is fiscally sustainable and delivers the
     highest-quality patient care to all Albertans, including indigenous peoples, regardless of
     where they reside in the province.
• The health and well-being of all Albertans is protected, supported and improved, and health
     inequities among underserved populations, including indigenous peoples, are reduced.
Key highlights and results achieved by the Ministry of Health in 2024-25 include:

    Reducing Surgical Wait         Alberta Health continued to reduce surgical wait times in 2024-25
    Times through Alberta          through initiatives under the Alberta Surgical Initiative (ASI),
    Surgical Initiative            including centralized referrals and expanded use of CSFs. By March
                                   31, 2025, 318,601 surgeries were completed, exceeding the target of
                                   310,000 and up 4.6 per cent from the previous year. About 74,928
                                   patients remained on the wait list, with 57.8 per cent within
                                   clinically recommended timelines.

                                   CSFs performed 64,500 surgeries (20.2 per cent of all scheduled
                                   procedures). Rural surgical volumes rose 4.4 per cent, and cancer
                                   surgeries increased 9.4 per cent, with 55 per cent completed within
                                   benchmark timelines.

    EHS Improvements               In 2024-25, Alberta invested $704 million in Emergency Health
                                   Service (EHS). EMS Return to Service initiatives reduced average
                                   hospital handover times. The replacement of end-of-life EMS
                                   vehicles was funded through the EMS Vehicle Capital Grant Program,
                                   with a total of $15 million in 2024-25 toward a $35-million, three-
                                   year investment in this program. This funding supports the
                                   purchase of new EHS vehicles and provides additional equipment to
                                   meet the needs of Albertans in both urban and rural areas of the
                                   province.

    Access to Cancer Care and      The Arthur J.E. Child Comprehensive Cancer Centre opened in
    Diagnostics                    Calgary on October 28, 2024. The $1.4-billion facility includes 160
                                   inpatient beds and 90 chemotherapy spaces. It will add a new
                                   clinical trial space in late 2025 and expand based on patient and
                                   clinical needs.

    Investment in Rural Health     To address rural health care needs, Alberta invested over $7.06
    Workforce                      million in 2024-25 through the Rural Health Professions Action Plan
                                   (RhPAP). An additional $2 million supported Indigenous second
                                   birth attendants. As of March 31, 2025, 53 NPs began providing care
                                   across zones, including four NPs on First Nation communities or
                                   Metis Settlements. Other investments in rural health workforce
                                   included: continued support for rural physician education
                                   programs; financial incentives for rural physicians, such as the Rural
                                   Remote Northern Program; AHS’ targeted recruitment of

                            internationally educated nurses; the launch of the Rural and Remote
                            Family Medicine Resident Physician Bursary Pilot Program; and
                            continued implementation of the Alberta Medical Association (AMA)
                            Agreement. The Indigenous Wellness Program Alternative
                            Relationship Plan was also established in 2014 to overcome
                            geographical, economic, political, cultural, and socio-historical

                            First Nations, Métis and Inuit communities. In 2024-25, 43 family

 Strengthening Primary      In 2024-25, the Government of Alberta continued to implement the
 Health Care                Modernizing Alberta’s Primary Health Care System (MAPS) initiative
                            that was launched in September 2022 to address urgent system
                            pressures and improve access to care. Following the release of MAPS
                            final reports in October 2023, the MAPS Two-Year Implementation
                            Plan was released in April 2024, which outlined a series of actions to
                            strengthen and stabilize primary health care in Alberta. In June
                            2024, an interactive dashboard publicly reported the progress of the
                            actions.

                            Budget 2024 invested $475 million to support the continued
                            implementation of the MAPS initiative, including: $100 million to
                            improve access to family physicians; $10 million for primary health
                            care initiatives in Indigenous communities; $17 million to further
                            develop a compensation model for nurse practitioners; $24.3 million
                            to support panel management and practice improvement activities;
                            and $30 million to address the needs of underserved populations.
                            The funding is dedicated to implementing the MAPS
                            recommendations aimed at strengthening primary health care
                            across the province.

 Health System Refocusing   In 2024-25, the Government of Alberta advanced transformation of
 Initiatives                the health system by establishing three sector-based PHAs for acute
                            care, mental health and addiction, and primary care – while work
                            was underway to establish stablished four Provincial Health
                            Agencies—ACA, ALA, PCA, and RA—to deliver more specialized and
                            integrated care across Alberta, with phased launches between 2024
                            and 2025. These agencies form the core of a unified provincial
                            health care framework designed to improve accountability,
                            integration, and patient-centred care.

 Digital Health             In 2024-25, the Government of Alberta continued to enhance digital
 Modernization              access to health information as part of its commitment to a person-
                            centred health care model. Progress included expanding the
                            information available through the MyHealth Records (MHR) portal
                            and integrating community primary care data into Alberta’s
                            Electronic Health Record, Alberta Netcare, Community Information
                            Integration and Central Patient Attachment Registry, and Connect
                            Care to support informed clinic decision-making. By March 31, 2025,
                            there were over 1.9 million MHRs users, with an average of 32,000
                            daily log ins. In addition, over 2,100 physician offices and 1,700

reduces unnecessary government oversight and emphasizes outcomes, to improve access to
government services, attract investment, support innovation and competitiveness, and grow
Alberta businesses.

Discussion and Analysis of Results
Actions that support the priorities of the Government of Alberta Strategic Plan



 Key Priority 2: Investing in Albertans


 Ensuring an accessible, responsive, and refocused health care system

 Refocusing the health care system to improve access and patient outcomes while empowering
 Alberta’s front-line health care workers. This refocusing work will create a unified health system
 with provincial sector-based health agencies that centre around four dedicated organizations:
 primary care, acute care, continuing care, and mental health and addiction. Alberta Health
 Services will continue to deliver acute care services, and an Integration Council will ensure the
 system is in alignment to better deliver health outcomes.

 Detailed reporting found on pages 32, 33, 35, 37 (Key Objectives 1.3, 1.4, 2.1, & 2.2)

 Improving access for underserved populations including First Nations, Métis, and Inuit peoples
 to quality and culturally appropriate health services that support improved health outcomes by
 promoting and exploring novel, innovative, and creative strategies.

 Detailed reporting found on pages 40, 46, 50, 51 (Key Objectives 2.4, 3.1, 3.3, & 3.4)

 Improving access to family doctors and health professionals. Government is working with the
 Alberta Medical Association to implement new payment models for family physicians while
 finding ways to reduce their administrative workload and allocating $57 million over three years
 to help doctors and nurse practitioners working in primary care manage costs of increased
 patient caseload. An additional $200 million over two years will improve access to family
 physicians and help ensure primary health care is available for every Albertan when and where
 they need it. Government is also emphasizing alternative models of care, such as access to team-
 based care and integration between primary health care and community care.

 Detailed reporting found on pages 29, 33 (Key Objectives 1.2 & 1.4)

 Improving Emergency Medical Services system response times, reducing emergency department
 and surgical wait times, and resolving laboratory and diagnostic services delays to ensure
 patients are receiving the appropriate level of care as well as empowering front-line workers to
 better deliver health care services.

 Detailed reporting found on page 20 (Key Objective 1.1)

 Strengthening and modernizing continuing care, primary health care, and digital health systems
 to increase choice, equitable and timely access, improving the quality of care for Albertans.

 Detailed reporting found on pages 20, 32, 39, 40, 42 (Key Objectives 1.1, 1.3, 2.3, 2.4, & 2.5)

 Addressing health care staffing challenges by investing $164 million in 2024-25 towards
 initiatives to train, recruit, and retain more health care professionals for Alberta. This work is
 supported by Alberta’s Health Workforce Strategy and the Rural Health Professions Action Plan,
 addressing specific challenges in remote and rural communities, as well as creating additional
 seats for physician education at Alberta’s medical schools.

 Detailed reporting found on page 29 (Key Objective 1.2)

 Addressing the province’s critical nursing shortage by making it easier for credentialed nurses
 from outside of Alberta/Canada to bring their skills to Alberta patients. Government is attracting
 internationally educated nurses by streamlining immigration processes for health care
 professionals and by addressing barriers they face when coming to Canada, including navigating
 regulatory requirements, assessment, and licensing processes, and having access to bridging
 education and clinical placements if needed.

 Detailed reporting found on pages 29, 33 (Key Objectives 1.2 & 1.4)

 Increasing access to maternal care by investing over $10 million over the next three years to
 develop and implement Alberta’s Midwifery Strategy, reducing pressure on hospitals and
 improving access to maternity care in rural and remote areas.

 Detailed reporting found on pages 46, 51 (Key Objectives 3.1 & 3.4)

 Investing $4.5 billion over three years in capital funding to maintain or expand health care
 facilities across the province. This includes funding for the redevelopment and expansion of the
 Red Deer Regional Hospital Centre, which will increase critical services and add capacity to one
 of the largest hospitals in central Alberta.

 Detailed reporting found on pages 46, 51 (Key Objectives 3.1 & 3.4)

 Forming lasting and meaningful partnerships with Indigenous communities:

 Working with the federal government and First Nations, Métis and Inuit communities to improve
 access for Indigenous Peoples no matter where they reside in the province. Working with the
 federal government, and First Nations, Métis and Inuit communities to improve access to key
 services such as education and healthcare for Indigenous peoples, no matter where they reside
 in the province.

Results Analysis



Outcome One: Enhanced timely access to an effective, safe and responsive publicly
funded health care system that provides Albertans with the necessary care when and
where they need it
Alberta’s government continues to make health care more accessible and responsive by building
health system capacity to support Albertans’ health and well-being throughout their lives, ensuring
an integrated and high-quality health care delivery that provides the highest levels of service to
Albertans and timely access to the most appropriate care in the most appropriate setting.

Key Objectives

1.1       Increased access to health care services for Albertans by improving the Emergency
          Medical Services system, reducing surgical wait times, decreasing emergency
          department wait times, reducing laboratory and diagnostic services delays, and
          improve primary health care across the province

A key priority for the Alberta government in 2024-25 was to continue strengthening the health care
system, making it more effective and accessible. Alberta Health remained focused on prioritizing
health system capacity to ensure a high-quality health system that meets patients’ needs and
provides access to the most appropriate care in the most suitable setting. This included building
surgical, and intensive care unit (ICU) capacities, as well as strengthening the health workforce.
Several initiatives were implemented to expand the capacity of Alberta’s publicly funded health
care system and minimize disruptions to patient care.

The Government of Alberta remained committed to increasing surgical capacity to keep pace with
demand and reduce the length of time Albertans waited for scheduled surgeries. Efforts continued
to improve the patient journey by streamlining care coordination and surgical pathways,
supporting family physicians with earlier specialist advice before consultations, and centralizing
referrals to match patients with the right surgeon faster. Alberta Health addressed urgent health
care needs while working towards a more resilient and efficient health care system for Albertans
with four key goals:

      •        Decreasing emergency department (ED) wait times
      •        Improving emergency medical services (EMS) response times
      •        Reducing wait times for surgeries
      •        Empowering front-line workers to deliver health care

The ministry implemented the Alberta Surgical Initiative (ASI), a surgical access improvement plan
with a patient-first focus to reduce surgery wait lists, wait times, and strengthen surgical services in
Alberta. This ensures that Albertans have surgeries within clinically recommended wait times. As
part of the ASI, Alberta Health worked with Alberta Health Services (AHS) to improve and
standardize the entire surgical journey by:

      •        Prioritizing surgeries and allocating operating room time according to the greatest need
      •        Streamlining referrals from primary care to specialists
      •        Increasing surgeries at underutilized operating rooms (mainly in rural areas)
      •        Providing lower-risk, routine surgeries through accredited chartered surgical facilities
               (CSFs) to help provide publicly funded insured services and extend existing capacity in
               hospitals



20                                                                           Health | Annual Report 2024–2025
                                                                                            Results Analysis




As part of ASI, the second operating room at the Rocky Mountain House Health Centre became
operational in April 2024. This project supports the government’s commitment to improve access
to timely surgical care for Albertans, particularly in rural communities.

The expansion project added 4,736 square feet of new space, including:

    •        A new operating room
    •        Six additional recovery beds
    •        A patient support area
    •        An upgraded medical device reprocessing department

This investment enables the facility to provide approximately 120 surgeries per month,
significantly enhancing rural surgical capacity and reducing the need for patients to travel long
distances for care.

ED Wait Times, EHS Performance, and Workforce Initiatives

The Government of Alberta implemented several initiatives in 2024-25 to reduce ED wait times by
increasing front-line staff capacity and improving patient flow. As of March 2025, there were 3,888
AHS nurses (including registered nurses, licensed practical nurses, and health care aides) working
in EDs —157 more than the previous year. Between April 1, 2024, and March 31, 2025, 83 ED
physicians were recruited, including:

    •        Physicians with a specialty designation
    •        Family physicians with additional emergency medicine certification
    •        Family physicians working in EDs without added credentials (Note: Covenant Health
             physicians are not included in these totals.)

The performance measure on ED wait times tracks the time to see a physician after being triaged on
arrival 90 per cent of the time in the 16 largest hospitals. The lower the number the better, as it
demonstrates patients are receiving timely assessment and treatment in EDs. In 2024-25, the wait
time to see a physician in the ED was 7.0 hours compared to 6.7 hours in 2023-24. The trend shows


Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                            21
Results Analysis



that patients were waiting 0.3 hours longer in the ED to see a physician in 2024-25 compared with
last year. The longer ED wait times was due to an increase in ED visits and the number of patients
with the highest level of acuity that require aggressive interventions such as patients with cardiac
arrest or major trauma.

An approximate 2.6 per cent increase in total ED visits and a 4.0 per cent increase in high-acuity
presentations were observed in 2024-25. In addition, hospital overcrowding and delays in
transferring patients to continuing care settings contributed to extended wait times.

To address these pressures, Alberta Health worked on initiatives such as:

     •       Expanding community care alternatives to divert non-urgent cases
     •       Improving access to continuing care beds
     •       Increasing hospital capacity
     •       Implementing streamlined patient discharge and treatment protocols in acute care.

EHS System Improvements

In 2024-25, the government spent $704 million to expand EHS and respond to the 98
recommendations from the Alberta Emergency Services Provincial Advisory Committee (AEPAC)
and the EHS Dispatch Review.

A new Alberta EMS Standing Committee was established and began meeting on April 24, 2024. This
committee includes frontline paramedics, municipal and Indigenous representatives, and other
community partners. Subcommittees address air ambulance, dispatch, ground ambulance, medical
first response, and workforce. The core committee meets monthly, while subcommittees meet
every eight weeks. These advisory groups are mandated until spring 2027.

EMS response times for 90 per cent of activity dropped between 2023-24 to 2024-25 for remote
communities and for rural community residents. However, EMS response times for 90 per cent of
activity were similar for communities with more than 3,000 residents and have slightly increased
for metro/urban communities over this same period. It is important to note that the very low call
volumes in the two rural and remote categories result in high sensitivity of response times to
changes in performance.

Alberta’s government is committed to ensuring EMS is available to Albertans when and where they
need it. Alberta Health and AHS continued working to divert low-acuity calls to 811, reduce EMS
offload times, and free up ambulances faster.

The EMS Return to Service initiative, launched in 2023, helped reduce handover time from
paramedics to ED staff. Average handover time dropped from 1.7 hours in December 2024 to 1.6
hours in February 2025. In January 2025, paramedics gained view-only access to Connect Care,
enabling real-time access to patient histories and improving on-site decision-making.

Surgical Volumes and Cancer Care Outcomes

In 2024-25, significant progress was achieved in expanding surgical capacity as part of the ASI
implementation. CSFs enabled a greater volume of publicly funded surgeries at no cost to patients
and continued to be an integral part of the ASI. There is a total of 48 CSF agreements, of which 20
pertain to oral and maxillofacial surgery (OMF) and are contracted directly with the surgeons
rather than through the CSF. Only one new agreement was established in the 2023-24 financial year
for Ear, Nose and Throat (ENT) (CLM208529), which is included in the total count. No new
agreements have been initiated for the 2024-25 financial year. CSFs remain an extension of hospital
capacity and are used in many other Canadian health systems. Under the Health Facilities Act, CSFs
providing publicly funded insured services must be accredited by the College of Physicians and
Surgeons of Alberta (CPSA) and have a signed service contract with AHS.

Through these dedicated efforts, as of March 31, 2025, approximately 318,601 surgeries had been
completed, surpassing the 2024-25 fiscal year target of 310,000. This represents an increase of
14,006 surgeries, or 4.6 per cent, compared with the 304,595 completed in 2023-24.

As of March 31, 2025, 74,928 cases were waiting for surgery. Of these cases, 57.8 per cent were
waiting within clinically recommended timelines. Efforts remain underway to reduce wait times
further and ensure equitable access across the province.

For the fiscal year 2024-25, 52,810 cases have been completed in rural acute care facilities. This
represents an increase in rural hospitals of 5.4 per cent in volumes completed in 2024-25
compared with fiscal year 2023-24, when 50,090 surgeries were completed.

Further, approximately 23,015 cancer surgeries were completed in 2024-25, a 9.4 per cent increase
compared with the same period in 2023-24. Nearly 55 per cent of cancer surgeries were completed
within clinically recommended wait times, helping to ensure patients receive timely access to
potentially life-saving procedures.

The Arthur J.E. Child Comprehensive Cancer Centre became fully operational on October 28, 2024.
The centre provides additional capacity for timely access to cancer diagnostics and treatment,
supporting optimal health outcomes for cancer patients. This new cancer centre in Calgary engages
patients at the centre of a multidisciplinary health system and offers a world-class environment for


Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                                        23
Results Analysis



cancer care. There are 160 inpatient beds and 90 chemotherapy treatment spaces available to meet
patient needs. All clinical areas are in use with flexibility for future growth as demand increases. A
new clinical trial space is expected to open later in 2025, with additional program expansions
guided by patient and clinical priorities.

In 2024-25, Alberta Health provided Wellspring Alberta with over $300,000 in funding to expand
non-clinical, psycho-social cancer care support services for remote and rural areas, including
Indigenous communities. With this grant, Wellspring Alberta is expanding both online and in-
person programming to improve access to supportive care, strengthen mental health outcomes, and
promote equity in the cancer care experience. Participants have reported improved well-being as a
result of the expanded services.

System Initiatives and Workforce Investments

The increase in completed surgical procedures this year reflects meaningful progress in expanding
Alberta’s surgical capacity and reducing wait times. This outcome demonstrates the Ministry of
Health’s continued commitment to the ASI. Building on this progress, the ministry implemented
system-wide improvements—including enhanced surgical referral pathways, prioritization based
on clinical urgency and continued use of CSFs-to deliver less-complex procedures efficiently.

The 2024-25 results for hip, knee and cataract surgical procedures included in this indicator show
an increase in the number of Albertans receiving these surgical procedures within national
benchmark wait times when compared with 2023-24 results.

Hip replacements increased from 62.4 per cent to 72.8 per cent, and knee replacements improved
from 53.3 per cent to 62.5 per cent. In addition to improved compliance with the national
benchmark for hips and knees, the overall number of surgeries for all three procedures has
increased at the same time.

In 2023–24, 59.7 per cent of cataract surgeries met the national benchmark, a five-percentage point
decline from the previous year. This drop is attributed to prioritizing patients who had waited the
longest, which lowered the percentage within target despite over 500 more surgeries being
completed on time. In 2024-25, performance improved to 62.8 per cent, reflecting recovery from
backlog reduction efforts.


24                                                                                         Health | Annual Report 2024–2025
                                                                                        Results Analysis



Alberta’s government continues to work with AHS to ensure more Albertans receive their surgeries
in a timely manner. Efforts are ongoing to implement an $8.2-million grant provided by Alberta
Health to further initiatives. The funds support AHS in implementing surgical smoothing
techniques—such as separating scheduled and urgent surgeries into different operating rooms—to
improve patient flow through Alberta’s busiest hospitals. AHS partnered with the Institute for
Healthcare Optimization (IHO) to implement these methods. As of 2024-25, six hospitals had
implemented the IHO methodology in Phase 1, with 10 more scheduled for Phase 2.

Under the ASI, Alberta performed more surgeries in rural communities and performed more
publicly funded surgeries at CSFs. In fiscal year 2024-25, an additional 2,180 surgeries were
completed in rural facilities compared with fiscal year 2023-24, while an additional 2,500 cases
were completed at CSFs. Overall, approaches like these, and others, facilitated the completion of
more surgeries in 2024-25 than any prior fiscal year, with a total of approximately 318,601 cases
completed and more than 13,000 additional surgeries compared to the year prior.

In 2024-25, Alberta Health also amended a prior grant to the CPSA to provide additional funding to
support expanding accreditation activities, in line with growth in CSF procedures. This builds on a
previous Alberta Health grant and helps CPSA continue meeting its accreditation obligations.

Alberta Health entered into several agreements with AHS to implement ASI-related projects:

    •        Invested over $3 million in the Rapid Access Clinics program to provide faster, more
             efficient access to care for musculoskeletal conditions.
    •        AHS launched a $2.7 million prehabilitation pilot, helping patients prepare for surgery
             with education and supports. As of March 2025, 2,258 referrals had been received and
             3,998 visits completed, with 84 per cent of participants reporting proved surgical
             recovery.
    •        Allocated over $800,000 to the Rural Surgical and Obstetrical Network of Alberta to
             enhance surgical and maternity care in rural communities.
    •        Provided over $1.1 million in funding to support CPSA regulatory oversight and
             accreditation related to CSFs and the monitoring of, and response to, safety incidents.
The Government of Alberta continued to address surgical workforce challenges to support system
expansion. As of March 31, 2025, there were 466 anesthesiologists registered in Alberta, up from
455 in 2024, representing a 2.4 per cent increase.

In July 2024, the Royal Alexandra Hospital experienced a temporary disruption in orthopedic
services due to staffing shortages. To address this, Alberta Health and AHS collaborated on a
strategic action plan to restore orthopedic surgery capacity. This included resuming procedures at
reduced volumes, prioritizing patients on wait lists, and continuing overnight-stay surgeries. AHS
also maintained recruitment efforts to restore full physician coverage and mitigate similar
disruptions elsewhere.

Province-wide, AHS continued working to increase operating room hours by addressing workforce
gaps. These efforts included targeted hiring and leadership development. As of January 31, 2025,
Cancer Care Alberta had recruited 41 full-time oncologists, 18 full-time hospitalists, and 115 clinical
operational staff. These investments reflect Alberta’s ongoing efforts to strengthen surgical and
cancer care teams and reduce wait times across the province.

Results Analysis



Capital Investments, Diagnostic Access, and ICU/NICU Expansion

The Government of Alberta continued investing in medical device reprocessing (MDR) as part of
broader efforts to reduce surgical backlogs and increase operating capacity. A total of $159 million
in funding was allocated across Phase 1 and Phase 2 of the MDR program over three years. These
funds are supporting renovations in nine hospitals: Foothills Medical Centre (Calgary), Westlock
Health Centre, Northern Lights Regional Hospital Centre (Fort McMurray), Sturgeon Community
Hospital (St. Albert), Royal Alexandra Hospital (Edmonton), Grey Nuns Community Hospital
(Edmonton), Misericordia Community Hospital (Edmonton), Alberta Children’s Hospital (Calgary),
and Drumheller Health Centre.

This capital program supports critical upgrades and expansions to sterilization and reprocessing
areas, enhancing compliance with safety standards and improving operational efficiency. All nine
projects advanced through planning and design in 2024-25. As part of the ASI, the province also
provided $313 million over three years for the ASI Capital Program to increase surgical capacity in
18 communities through 39 projects. These initiatives will deliver 21 new and 49 upgraded
operating rooms across both urban and rural locations and include both Alberta Infrastructure-led
and AHS-led projects.

The ministry continued to make strategic infrastructure investments to support the delivery of
publicly funded health services. Significant advancements in 2024-25 included:

     •       Arthur J.E. Child Comprehensive Cancer Centre: A $1.4-billion investment enabled the
             opening of this state-of-the-art centre on October 28, 2024, offering 160 inpatient beds,
             90 chemotherapy spaces, 100-plus exam rooms, 12 radiation vaults (plus 3 shelled for
             future growth), clinical trials space, and full outpatient services.
     •       Red Deer Regional Hospital Centre Redevelopment: Supported by a $1.8-billion
             investment, this project includes a new inpatient tower, expanded ED, and surgical suite
             renovations. Construction of the inpatient tower began in 2024.
     •       Rockyview General Hospital: An $84-million expansion project was completed in
             November 2024, improving intensive care, coronary care, and endoscopy capacity. The
             redevelopment increased the ICU and CCU to 25 single patient rooms (13 ICU and 12
             CCU) from the previous 17 and moved the unit into a space that better supports patient
             and family centred care.
     •       Budget 2024 included $313 million over three years for the Alberta Surgical Initiative
             Capital Program to increase surgical capacity in 18 communities through 39 projects.
             The following projects were completed and became operational:
             o The second operating room at Rocky Mountain House Health Centre became
                operational in April 2024.
             o An operating room upgrade at the McCaig Tower in Foothills Medical Centre in
                Calgary was completed in Dec 2024.
             o Upgrade to two operating rooms, Medical Device Reprocessing Department (MDRD)
                enhancements and inpatient room renovations at Chinook Regional Hospital in
                Lethbridge was completed in Jan 2025.


In Budget 2024, the government allocated $512 million over three years, including $172 million in
2024-25 through the Capital Maintenance and Renewal Program, to maintain existing
infrastructure. The Rural Health Facilities Revitalization Program continued to deliver smaller-scale
renovations in rural communities, helping maintain access to safe, modern care across the province.
Notable projects included renovations to the emergency department at Taber Health Centre

completion), laboratory renovations at Fairview Health Complex (complete), and the purchase and

This metric tracks the percentage of scheduled surgeries performed in CSFs. More scheduled
surgeries performed in CSFs helps reduce wait times by increasing surgery volumes and improving
access so that Albertans receive their procedures where and when they are needed. CSFs continue
to be an integral part of the ASI implementation to enable increased volumes of publicly funded
surgeries at no cost to patients. In 2024-25, there were 38 existing CSFs, and accredited CSFs
provided around 64,500 surgeries (20.2 per cent of publicly scheduled surgeries). This is an
increase of around 2,050 surgeries from 2023-24 (3.3 per cent increase).

This performance indicator reflects the estimated number of scheduled and emergency surgeries

In 2024-25, 318,601 surgeries were performed in Alberta, up by almost 15,000 from 304,595
surgeries in 2023-24. This represents an increase of 4.6 per cent and can in part be credited to the
ASI — the government’s plan to increase the number of surgeries and ensure Albertans receive
their procedures within clinically recommended wait times. Additionally, while the number of
surgeries performed in CSFs continued to rise, this year’s growth reflects a more modest increase
compared to the larger increases seen in previous years. The volume of surgeries performed at
CSFs is expected to continue to increase over the coming years as more CSFs become operational.

 *MRI targets: Emergency department patients - within 24 hours; hospital patients - 24-72 hours (condition dependent);

The percentage of patients receiving scans within provincial wait time targets remained consistent
for CT scans and declined marginally for MRIs. This trend reflects factors that continue to impact
service delivery, including rising demand and case complexity driven by Alberta’s aging population,
equipment and site infrastructure constraints from further scaling, and workforce recruitment and
retention challenges.

To improve access to diagnostic imaging, the Government of Alberta allocated $15 million in 2024-
25 to community diagnostic imaging providers. With this investment, AHS expects to perform
41,000 additional CT scans and 12,000 additional MRI scans. As a result:

     •       Median CT wait time dropped from 28 to 21 days
     •       Median MRI wait time dropped from 57 to 53 days
     •       AHS delivered approximately 540,000 CT and 230,000 MRI scans in 2024-25
Wait times for community lab services were improved in the following ways:

     •       In-person waits dropped by 36 per cent (35.2 to 22.6 minutes)
     •       Next available appointment wait times decreased by 54 per cent (from 9.3 to 4.4 days)
     •       Patient visits increased by 11 per cent (from 3.94M to 4.38M)
Following the transition of services from DynaLIFE to Alberta Precision Laboratories (APL) in
2023-24, wait times have consistently met or exceeded targets. APL continues to increase staffing
and appointment capacity, and 92 per cent of respondents to a recent patient survey reported
satisfaction with their lab experience.

In 2024, Alberta signed a three-year, approximately $54-million bilateral agreement with the
federal government under the National Strategy for Drugs for Rare Diseases. This will provide
earlier access to emerging rare disease drugs, as well as improve diagnostics and screening. Of the
total, up to $5.4 million annually is allocated to improving rare disease screening and testing.

As part of a $283-million four-year grant through the 2022-26 Business Plan, the government
continued to expand ICU baseline funding to support the development and operation of an
additional 50 permanent ICU beds across the province. As of November 2024, AHS added one more
ICU bed at Foothills Medical Centre, bringing the provincial total to 224 ICU beds.

To address neonatal capacity pressures, an additional 21 neonatal ICU beds were approved. As of
March 31, 2025, six of these beds were operational, increasing Alberta’s total NICU capacity to 315
beds. The new beds were distributed as follows:

      •        3 in Calgary Zone at Peter Lougheed Centre
      •        2 at Grey Nuns Community Hospital and 1 at Misericordia Community Hospital in
               Edmonton Zone
EHS Workforce Development and Response Capacity

Alberta Health worked with AHS to implement workforce strategies aimed at reducing vacancies
among non-casual EHS staff. Between Q1 and Q3 of 2024-25:

      •        The number of primary and advanced care paramedics increased from 3,176 to 3,220;
               and
      •        AHS hired 419 new staff, including 295 paramedics—164 into full-time roles.

To strengthen rural response capacity:

      •        The number of emergency medical responders (EMRs) in the public EMS system grew
               from 80 in November 2022 to 219 by December 2024.
      •        Significant growth occurred in the North Zone and South Zone, with increases across all
               zones.
      •        EMRs continue to serve lower-acuity transfer and response roles, particularly in rural
               and remote areas.
EHS Fleet Expansion

To ensure sufficient vehicle supply, Alberta invested $15 million in 2024-25 toward a $35-million
three-year commitment under the EMS Vehicle Capital Grant Program. This funded the purchase of
new EHS vehicles and provided additional equipment to meet the needs of Albertans in both urban
and rural areas.

As of February 18, 2025, EHS transport volumes were approximately 15 per cent higher than
February 2023, highlighting the importance of fleet growth to meet rising demand.

1.2       Ensure equitable and timely access to health services across the province, including in
          remote, rural and Indigenous communities, by implementing workforce strategies and
          policies to attract, recruit and retain health professionals, and modernizing critical
          capabilities in the delivery of health care services

In 2024-25, Alberta Health advanced efforts to improve maternal health outcomes and reduce
strain on acute care services through the development and implementation of Alberta’s Midwifery
Strategy. Released in February 2025, the strategy provides a long-term framework to guide
investments of up to $10 million in funding over three years to expand midwifery services across
the province, particularly in rural and remote communities with limited access. As of January 2025,


Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                        29
Results Analysis



181 midwives were practising across Alberta, a 26 per cent increase from 2020. In 2024-25, the
first $1.4-million grant under this strategy was established to support the development of
Indigenous second birth attendants, further supporting culturally safe maternity care.

Alberta Health provided over $7.06 million in funding to the Rural Health Professions Action Plan
(RhPAP) in 2024-25. Although the Rural Education Supplement and Integrated Doctor Experience
Program ended March 31, 2024, efforts through RhPAP successfully committed 20 physicians for a
total of 59 years of service in rural Alberta. In 2024-25, RhPAP spent $445,000 to fulfil ongoing
contractual commitments.

Strengthening Health Workforce Capacity and Retention

Alberta Health and the Alberta Medical Association (AMA) completed a joint request for proposals
to review the Rural Remote Northern Program, which provides incentives to attract physicians to
underserved communities. Recommendations from the review are expected by March 31, 2025, and
will inform future improvements. The Government of Alberta also continued to implement its
Health Workforce Strategy, released in December 2024, to strengthen Alberta’s capacity to recruit
and retain health professionals. The number of physicians practising in Alberta increased by 474
(4.2 per cent) from December 2023 to December 2024, demonstrating meaningful year-over-year
growth. This progress builds on the 2023–24 investments in workforce infrastructure and reflects
Alberta’s continued commitment to sustainable physician supply and improved patient access.

Modernizing Primary Care Models

Alberta Health collaborated with the AMA to develop a new Primary Care Physician Compensation
Model (PCPCM) to support family medicine and rural generalist physicians. Implemented on April
1, 2025, this new model is designed to ensure Alberta’s family doctors are competitively
compensated, while delivering patient-centred care, and aims to enhance access to primary care
across the province, particularly in underserved areas.

The PCPCM is structured to:

     •       Encourage physicians to grow their patient panels and support full-time practice.
     •       Reward high-volume, high-quality care through incentives for after-hours access,
             technology use, and team-based service delivery.

As of March 31, 2025, 1059 physicians were approved to apply for PCPCM, of which 818 physicians
submitted PCPCM applications to Alberta Health. This initiative represents a shift from fee for
service to a more integrated, outcome-oriented approach, in line with national best practices and
aligned with Alberta Health Workforce Strategy.

Nurse Practitioner Primary Care Program (NPPCP)

The Nurse Practitioner Primary Care Program (NPPCP) launched on April 1, 2024, as part of
broader efforts to expand access through team-based care. The program provides compensation
that supports nurse practitioners (NPs) so they can practise comprehensive patient care
autonomously, whether in their own practices or in existing primary care settings. As of March 24,
2025:

     •       53 NPs were practising under the NPPCP, of which 20 are practising in rural
             communities.
     •       Over 31,905 patients had been seen through the program.


30                                                                        Health | Annual Report 2024–2025
                                                                                       Results Analysis



    •        Nearly 21,282 patients were paneled to NPPCP providers as their most responsible
             practitioner.

This early uptake highlights the program’s potential to reduce access barriers and relieve pressure
on the physician system.

Supporting Indigenous Health Access and Equity

The Indigenous Patient Navigator Grant Program that distributed $7.6 million over three years,
continued to address access barriers and navigation challenges faced by First Nations, Métis, and
Inuit patients and communities. The program provides financial support to recruit and retain
patient navigators who assist individuals in accessing primary health care and community services.

In 2024-25:

    •        Alberta Health received 35 applications under the program.
    •        18 Indigenous Patient Navigators were funded across the province.

Building on this success, the Government of Canada contributed an additional $6.3 million over
three years to launch a second round of the Navigator Program through the Bilateral Indigenous
Patient Navigator Grant Program. Combined, total funding under both streams reached $13.9
million over three years.

Through the bilateral program, eligible applicants may receive:

    •        Up to $150,000 annually (or $450,000 over three years)
    •        Support covering salary, benefits, and direct program costs

These navigators continue to play a vital role in advancing culturally safe care and improving
Indigenous patients' experience with Alberta’s health system.

Rural Health Priorities

In October 2024, Alberta Health reaffirmed its commitment to rural and remote care by publishing
the Rural Health Action Plan (RHAP), which is aligned with ongoing system refocusing initiatives,
including the Modernizing Alberta’s Primary Health Care System (MAPS) initiative and Alberta
Health Workforce Strategy.

The RHAP outlines five rural priorities:

    •        Workforce
    •        Access
    •        Models of care
    •        Community care
    •        Prevention and wellness

These priorities reflect continued investment in rural health system readiness and equity of access
for smaller communities.


Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                    31
Results Analysis



Investing in Future Rural Physicians

As of October 2024, the Government of Alberta launched the two-year $16 million Rural and
Remote Family Medicine Resident Physician Bursary Program. The program provides financial
support to family medicine residents at the University of Alberta and University of Calgary who
commit to practising in rural or remote Alberta for a minimum of three years following residency.

In its first year:

      •        Applications opened on January 6, 2025
      •        Approximately $2.8 million was spent to support 21 residents
      •        Bursary recipients are expected to begin service placements in 2025–26

This program builds on Alberta’s ongoing investment in rural physician attraction and aligns with
the broader Health Workforce Strategy by strengthening the rural physician pipeline early in
medical training.

Supporting Community Health Centres

The underserved grant program provided $30 million in funding to support four community health
centres (CHCs): The Alex ($11.6 million) and CUPS Calgary Society ($6.6 million) located in Calgary,
and Jasper Place Wellness Centre ($4.8 million) and Radius Community Health and Healing ($7
million) located in Edmonton. Funds from the grant agreements executed in February 2025 will
enhance the ability of CHCs to deliver team-based, community focused care by:

      •        Enhancing access to primary care for underserved and marginalized populations
      •        Expanding mental health and outreach services
      •        Supporting walk-in and team-based models of care
      •        Providing critical services such as housing navigation, recovery support, and food access
               programs

Modernizing Pharmacy Infrastructure

To support medication safety and modernize infrastructure, the Government of Alberta committed
$57 million over three years to renovate and upgrade 33 pharmacies across the province. This
pharmacy capital program ensures compliance with new sterile compounding standards set by the
National Association of Pharmacy Regulatory Authorities, adopted by the Alberta College of
Pharmacy. This initiative helps ensure consistent access to safe, high-quality pharmaceutical
services in hospitals and care centres, especially in rural and remote areas.

1.3       Ensure processes for resolving patient concerns are effective, streamlined, and
          consistent across the province

The ministry worked to ensure that the patient complaints process remained fair, responsive, and
accessible. AHS implemented targeted enhancements to its complaint management system, with a
focus on strengthening mechanisms for reviewing and addressing feedback from patients and
families in a timely and effective manner.

As part of the Alberta government’s continued efforts to strengthen patient-centred care,
transparency, and accountability, the ministry implemented recommendations from the Office of
the Alberta Health Advocate and Mental Health Patient Advocate. Building on progress made in
2023-24, this work included:

      •        Advancing standardized follow-up protocols for patient complaints
      •        Establishing mandatory information-sharing requirements to support a consistent
