Strengthening the Voice of Patients and Caregivers

In alignment with the MAPS Two-Year Implementation Plan, Alberta Health is supporting the
launch of Care Opinion Canada, an online platform designed to collect public feedback on health
care experiences. The initiative stems from the MAPS advisory panels’ recommendation to
strengthen the voice of patients and caregivers within the health system. In March 2024, $704,000
in MAPS initiative funding was provided to support the pilot launch of Care Opinion by amending
an existing grant with Imagine Citizens Network (ICN). ICN has achieved several key milestones,
including signing a licensing agreement to establish Care Opinion Canada, hiring core staff, and
securing early adopters for a successful pilot of the program.

Improving Culturally Appropriate Complaint Resolution

Building on previous work, the Alberta government continued to improve the process for
addressing Indigenous patient complaints and expanding access to culturally appropriate, high-
quality health services for First Nations, Métis, and Inuit peoples.

In 2024-25, the government hired Alberta’s first Indigenous Patient Safety Investigator and
Advocate, Anthony Cardinal. The role was established to ensure Indigenous patients can advocate
for themselves while also receiving support from a trusted voice when navigating the health
system. The investigator’s mandate includes:

      •        Listening to and supporting Indigenous patients and families in addressing their
               concerns
      •        Ensuring concerns are addressed in culturally safe, appropriate ways
      •        Addressing experiences of racism in the health system
      •        Supporting implementation of the Alberta Health Charter
This appointment aligns with recommendations made by the Indigenous Primary Health Care
Implementation Panel and reinforces Alberta’s commitment to improving trust, access, and
culturally safe care for Indigenous communities.

1.4       Increase oversight to improve safety while reducing red tape within the health system by
          streamlining processes and reducing duplication

As part of its ongoing commitment to regulatory efficiency, the Government of Alberta implemented
red tape reduction initiatives to remove unnecessary administrative barriers and modernize
outdated practices. This included restructuring and modernizing health legislation by removing
unnecessary requirements to access services, and by ensuring that policies reflect modern models
of care. These efforts reduced administrative barriers, strengthened front-line capacity, and
improved patient outcomes.



Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                      33
Results Analysis



Through collaboration with the ministries of Service Alberta and Red Tape Reduction, Health, and
Technology and Innovation, the Government of Alberta is advancing the health card modernization
initiative, which aims to address fraudulent use of the Alberta Health Care Insurance Plan (AHCIP).
For example, there are currently more than five million registered paper health cards despite
Alberta’s population being approximately 4.7 million. Options to modernize the health care card,
while improving both its’ security and durability, are under consideration.

The Body of Rules Agreement Netcare Dialog (BRAND) application has been developed to
streamline the operation and management of Alberta Netcare. Once launched, BRAND will result in
a simplified electronic agreement process, significantly reducing administrative burden for
physicians.

Through these targeted initiatives, the ministry remains committed to:

     •       Enhancing timely and equitable access to care
     •       Expanding patient choice
     •       Improving overall quality of services

As these reforms are integrated into a refocused and unified provincial health system, continuous
performance monitoring will ensure transparency, accountability, and better outcomes for
Albertans.

                                                                                       Results Analysis



Outcome Two: A modernized and refocused health care system that delivers the highest-
quality patient care to all Albertans, including Indigenous Peoples, regardless of where
they reside in the province, and is fiscally sustainable
Alberta is refocusing the health care system to prioritize patient care and empower frontline health
care workers, including those serving rural, remote and Indigenous communities, to deliver the
highest quality health care.

The Ministry of Health is reporting on advancements in palliative and end-of-life care, along with
strengthened licensing and monitoring, as key components of a modernized and fiscally sustainable
health care system that delivers high-quality care to all Albertans.

Key Objectives

2.1       Improve patient outcomes and access through the creation of dedicated organizations
          for acute care, continuing care, and primary care, within a single functioning health
          care system

The Government of Alberta continues to focus on controlling the rising costs of health care while
providing Albertans with the health care services they expect and deserve. In 2024-25, the ministry
worked with health system partners to balance between fiscal responsibility and necessary
investments. The goal is a strong, responsive, and refocused health system that supports economic
growth and world-class health care, with the capacity to respond to emerging public health
challenges.

On October 31, 2024, the Government of Alberta introduced legislative amendments through the
Health Statutes Amendment Act to continue building a health care system that responds to the
changing needs of Albertans. The Health Statutes Amendment Act ensures Albertans have a health
system that prioritizes their need to:

      •        Find a primary care provider
      •        Get urgent care without long wait times
      •        Access high-quality continuing care
      •        Receive mental health and addiction services across the province
These legislative amendments enabled a unified health care system with four provincial health
agencies (PHAs). As part of this refocusing, responsibility for health system planning transitioned
from AHS to Alberta Health. As part of the transition, Alberta Health led a province-wide health
system assessment that looked at current and future health needs, services, workforce, and
infrastructure. The assessment helped shape funding decisions and promoted long-term planning.

The Ministries of Health, Mental Health and Addiction, and Seniors, Community and Social Services
created four PHAs:

      •        Acute Care Alberta (ACA) (operational as of April 1, 2025)
      •        Assisted Living Alberta (ALA) (legal entity as of April 1, 2025)
      •        Primary Care Alberta (PCA) (operational as of February 1, 2025)
      •        Recovery Alberta (RA) (legal entity as of July 1, 2024).




Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                     35
Results Analysis



Ministerial orders were signed early to support smooth transitions. Recovery Alberta is led by the
Ministry of Mental Health and Addiction and not covered in this report. Additionally, the
responsibility for the stand-up of the continuing care agency was transferred to the Ministry of
Seniors, Community, and Social Services in fall 2024.

AHS will continue to have a strong role in the refocused system by concentrating on delivering
acute care services. New regulations have been introduced to formalize the designation and
oversight of health services delivery organizations and to establish the AHS Provincial Health
Corporation.

Each PHA has a specific focus. ACA manages hospital care, urgent care centres, CSFs, EHS and
cancer care. ALA oversees continuing care, including care homes and home supports. PCA
coordinates primary health services and works to connect Albertans with care teams. RA
provides recovery-oriented mental health and addiction services and correctional health services in
Alberta.

A health shared services entity (SSE) is being established to provide corporate and support services
for all PHAs and health services delivery organizations. It will be independent and is expected to be
operational by September 2025.

As the system establishes additional organizations, tools are being put in place to make sure it
works collaboratively. The Integration Council was established that looks at system-wide strategic
goals, finds efficiencies, and removes barriers to system coordination. The council began meeting
regularly in March 2024. Membership includes ministers, deputy ministers, PHA and AHS leaders,
and senior executives from HQCA and the Centre of Recovery Excellence.

To enhance local decision-making, Alberta Health will establish 14 regional advisory councils
(RACs) and one Indigenous Advisory Council. RACs and the Indigenous Advisory Council are
guiding Alberta’s health care transformation by ensuring community perspectives are heard. RACs
will provide advice and recommendations on workforce, capital, and clinical service planning,
identify local health issues, and support locally driven solutions. The Indigenous Advisory Council
will bring forward Indigenous priorities, inform culturally appropriate care, and explore integrating
traditional practices. In 2024-25:

     •       Nearly 500 candidates applied and approximately 100 additional individuals were
             nominated by various stakeholders for minister’s consideration to appoint
     •       210 interviews were held
     •       Operational frameworks, policies and other materials were created
     •       Training and onboarding materials for council members were developed
     •       A custom system was built to support council work.


The following other changes have supported the Health Care System Refocusing Initiative:

     •       The management and oversight of all EHS — including ambulances and paramedic
             care — will be transferred from AHS to ACA later in 2025. These changes will help
             refocus EHS to better meet the needs of Albertans and ensure improved access to the
             best health care possible.
     •       Cancer care services will also move out of AHS under the oversight of ACA in 2025.



36                                                                        Health | Annual Report 2024–2025
                                                                                          Results Analysis



      •        Services for organ and tissue donation and transplantation will also move out of AHS
               and under the oversight of ACA.
      •        ALA will be operational by fall 2025, aligning medical and non-medical care, increasing
               continuing care spaces, reducing wait times, and offering wraparound supports for
               Albertans who require various levels of care. ALA will support seniors, individuals with
               disabilities, and vulnerable populations needing both temporary and long-term care.
      •        Effective January 6, 2025, the Ministry of Seniors, Community and Social Services
               assumed responsibility for the governance of Alberta’s continuing care sector; however,
               some functions remain in Health, including the License and Compliance Monitoring
               Branch, Continuing Care Information Systems and Medical Assistance in Dying.
In 2024-25, the Procurement and System Optimization Secretariat, Alberta Health division,
continued to work closely with AHS Contracting, Procurement and Supply Management and
program areas to develop and implement contract assignment approaches that will enable the
PHAs to continue the provision of appropriate health care and support to Albertans without
disruption.

A total of 82 contracts were assigned from AHS to PCA effective February 1st, 2025, as per
Ministerial Order 802/2025. These contracts were part of the initial wave of contracts that were
directly related to the transition of programs and services which included Health Link, the
Midwifery Program and the Provincial Primary Health Care Program.

In February 2025, the ministry reported $22.5 million in operational site and zone rebates and
forecasted $288 million in pharmacy and cancer care rebates and $38 million in surgical care
rebates. Additionally, $5.8 million in funding supported education, training, fellowships, and
research – key components of clinical programs enabled through these realized benefits. These
efficiencies support the long-term sustainability of Alberta’s health system and reflect the
ministry’s ongoing efforts to modernize care delivery and optimize resources.

2.2       Engage the health workforce, patients, families, caregivers, and system stakeholders to
          understand front-line and local perspectives to effectively implement the Health Care
          System Refocusing Initiative

In 2024-25, Alberta Health implemented a layered, province-wide engagement strategy to ensure
the voices of Albertans informed the Health Care System Refocusing Initiative. This approach
included in-person public sessions, targeted discussions with Indigenous and francophone
communities, health workforce engagement, and online engagement. The goal was to gather
diverse perspectives and lived experiences to guide the modernization of Alberta’s health care
system and support sustainable, effective, and locally responsive care.

Public Engagement

Between January 24 and April 12, 2024, the Government of Alberta hosted 65 in-person public
engagement sessions across the province. A second round of sessions began on January 21, 2025;
by March 31, 2025, 54 of the 63 planned sessions had been completed, with the remaining 10 held
in April and May 2025. These two-hour sessions began with a short presentation on the system
refocusing effort, followed by facilitated small-group discussions. Conversations were guided by six
questions exploring strengths in the local health system, existing gaps, collaboration opportunities,
mechanisms for accountability, local decision-making, and supports needed for health workers,
patients, caregivers, and communities.




Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                       37
Results Analysis



In 2024, a total of 2,850 Albertans attended the sessions, including 192 who participated in the final
10 sessions held in April 2024. Key themes that emerged included the importance of timely access
to care; clear and transparent communication; strong support for the health workforce; the unique
needs of rural, remote, and Indigenous communities; the value of local decision-making; two-way
accountability; and improved coordination across the system.

To maintain ongoing communication, Alberta Health distributed four e-newsletters between
January and March 2025 to over 7,500 subscribers, totalling 27,935 emails. Topics included
announcements and engagement opportunities related to ALA and emergency services.

Indigenous and Francophone Engagement

From April to June 2024, the Government of Alberta conducted 18 Indigenous information
gathering sessions — four in person and 14 virtual. The in-person sessions were hosted in locations
aligned with the Medicine Wheel (north, south, east, and west regions). These sessions were
facilitated with the support of the Indigenous Health Division and guided by seven culturally
informed questions addressing cultural practices, health partnerships, equitable access,
recruitment, accountability, and system improvements.

Feedback from First Nations, Métis, and Inuit participants highlighted significant barriers to
accessing care, limited Indigenous engagement and representation, a lack of culturally safe services,
insufficient health promotion and education, gaps in mental health and addiction supports, limited
continuity in transitions of care, and concerns related to treaty rights and information-sharing
agreements. An Indigenous-specific online feedback form was also available between April 30 and
July 5, 2024.

To ensure French-speaking Albertans had equitable opportunities to participate, Alberta Health, in
collaboration with the Association canadienne-française de l'Alberta, Réseau santé albertain, and
the Francophone Secretariat, hosted two French-language engagement sessions: an in-person
session in Edmonton on June 17, 2024, and a virtual session on June 20, 2024. These were
conducted entirely in French, with all materials provided in French. The sessions reinforced key
public engagement themes and surfaced unique priorities, including improved access to health care
in French, community governance, and language promotion. A French-language online feedback
form was available from June 6 to July 5, 2024.

Online Engagement

In addition to in-person and targeted sessions, Alberta Health provided online feedback tools in
English, French, and Indigenous-specific formats. Two public feedback windows were held:
November 10, 2023, to January 15, 2024, and January 23 to April 21, 2024. Over 18,000 responses
were received from Albertans, with a higher representation of respondents identifying as female
and aged 35 to 54 years compared with the general population. Respondents prioritized issues
related to workforce development, improvements to service delivery, health system structure and
governance, partnerships, integration of technology, and resource allocation. While the French and
Indigenous online forms had smaller sample sizes, the themes mirrored those raised during the in-
person sessions.

Health Workforce Engagement

Alberta Health directly engaged health care providers through public sessions and targeted
communications. Many providers participated in the in-person sessions from January to April 2024
and again between January and April 2025. Workforce feedback was continuously integrated into
system planning.


38                                                                        Health | Annual Report 2024–2025
                                                                                            Results Analysis



The Alberta Health Change Management Program played a key role in supporting communications
with staff affected by the transition to new PHAs. This included regular updates, reference
documents, such as frequently asked questions and fact sheets, onboarding resources, and in-
person and virtual engagement opportunities. The program also facilitated ongoing collaboration
between AHS, ACA, and PCA, ensuring alignment and minimizing disruption to service delivery.

Additional engagement activities included seven regional sessions with health foundations between
October 29 and December 11, 2024. These were held both virtually and in person, with 59 of
Alberta’s 70 health foundations participating, which includes an increasing participating rate to 84
per cent. Outreach was also conducted with medical associations, First Nations, Métis and Inuit
health leaders, university researchers, and members of the PCA and ACA medical leadership.
Physician-specific town halls were organized to enable direct dialogue with leadership teams.

Outcomes and Next Steps

The feedback received through these engagement efforts was shared with the Minister of Health,
the Minister of Mental Health and Addiction, and system leaders across the province. Alberta Health
remains committed to demonstrating how this input is being used to guide decisions, develop
policies, and shape the implementation of the refocused health care system. A comprehensive
“What We Heard” report is available through the Lead the Way platform, and future updates will
continue to reflect the voices of Albertans who contribute to this important work.

2.3   Modernize and transform the continuing care system to improve access and shift to
      more care in the community

The Ministry of Health is reporting on progress in palliative and end-of-life care, as well as licensing
and monitoring, to support the transformation of Alberta’s continuing care system and improve access
to community-based services.

Responsibility for the stand-up of the continuing care agency was transferred to the Ministry of
Seniors, Community and Social Services in fall 2024. In 2024-25, the Ministry of Health made
significant strides toward strengthening and modernizing Alberta’s continuing care, primary health
care, and digital health systems. Alberta’s government maintained its commitment to shift care to
community-based settings and expand access to high-quality continuing care services.

Palliative and End-of-Life Care

In Alberta, palliative and end-of-life care is offered in a range of settings including, but not limited
to, acute care hospitals, outpatient clinics, primary care provider settings, continuing care homes
(including type C- hospice), EMS, and home and community care settings.

To address the growing needs among Alberta’s aging population, the Government of Alberta has
prioritized investments that enhance access to palliative and end-of-life care as part of its
continuing care transformation.

These investments are aimed at ensuring Albertans receive compassionate, appropriate, and
dignified care at all stages of life, including end-of-life.




Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                           39
Results Analysis



Licensing and Monitoring in Continuing Care

To strengthen accountability, transparency, and oversight in Alberta’s continuing care system, the
Government of Alberta introduced a new legislative framework:

      •       On April 1, 2024, the Continuing Care Act and associated regulations—Continuing Care
              Regulation and Continuing Care (Ministerial) Regulation—came into effect. This
              legislative update marked a major milestone in transforming Alberta’s continuing care
              system.
      •       The Act enables Alberta Health to enhance licensing, monitoring, and compliance
              processes across all levels of continuing care, including home and community care,
              supportive living, and palliative services. These enhanced oversight mechanisms are
              intended to ensure quality, safety, and equitable access to services.

Additionally, ALA, expected to become operational by fall 2025, will align medical and non-medical
services, expand access to continuing care, and support system accountability through integrated
service delivery and performance monitoring.

The performance metric 2.b, included in the 2024-27 Business Plan, pertains to Continuing Care
which has been transitioned to the Ministry of Seniors, Community and Social Services. Hence, this
data will not be discussed in this report.

Note: The result is for Acute Care data submissions

The above performance measure estimates the percentage of medical patients with an unplanned
readmission to hospital within 30 days of being discharged. A lower percentage means fewer
patients have been readmitted to hospital within one month of discharge. A high rate of
readmissions increases costs and may mean the health system is not performing as well as it could
be. Although readmission may involve many factors, lower readmission rates may show that
Albertans are supported by discharge planning and continuity of care. Results exclude hospital
admissions for surgery, pregnancy, childbirth, mental health diseases and disorders, palliative care,
and cancer therapy. The desired result is to decrease the percentage of unplanned readmissions to
hospital within 30 days of discharge.

In 2024-25, the percentage of medical patients with an unplanned hospital readmission within 30
days of discharge from hospital was 12.7 per cent, identical to as that of previous years.

2.4       Strengthen and modernize Alberta’s primary health care system in partnership with
          Albertans and Indigenous communities by implementing innovations to ensure access
          to safe, timely and appropriate primary health care services in the community


40                                                                                  Health | Annual Report 2024–2025
                                                                                        Results Analysis



A strong primary health care system is foundational for better health care for Albertans. In 2024-
25, the Government of Alberta took significant steps to stabilize and strengthen primary health care
so that every Albertan can access timely and high-quality care when and where they need it.

To advance this goal, Alberta continued implementing recommendations from the MAPS initiative.
These actions support the province’s commitment to building a more patient-centred, integrated,
and equitable health care system.

In response to the MAPS Strategic Advisory Panel and the Indigenous Advisory Panel final reports,
several initiatives were launched in 2024-25:

    •        $57 million over three years was provided to family doctors and NPs to help manage
             rising patient care costs.
    •        A three-year grant of $704,000 in 2024-25 supported Imagine Citizens’ pilot of Care
             Opinion, an online platform for public feedback on health care experiences.
    •        A $400,000 grant supported the continued operation and expansion of the Alberta Find
             a Doctor website, including family physicians and NPs not affiliated with Primary Care
             Networks.
    •        $5 million over two years was invested in the Primary Care Fund to reduce
             administrative burden, improve access to primary care, and enhance team-based care.
These investments are addressing urgent pressures and helping to stabilize Alberta’s primary
health care system.

The Government of Alberta remains committed to improving access to culturally safe and
appropriate care for First Nations, Métis and Inuit peoples. In 2024-25, $27.6 million was allocated
to advance Indigenous health equity through two major grants:

    •        $7.6 million over three years funded the Navigator Program, supporting 18
             organizations (up to $450,000 each) to assist Indigenous patients in navigating the
             health system, reducing barriers, and improving primary care access. The funding aligns
             with Alberta’s new Indigenous Health Division and ongoing efforts to support
             Indigenous-led health care solutions. Following the success of the program, the
             Government of Canada contributed an additional $6.3 million over three years in
             funding through the Bilateral Indigenous Patient Navigator Grant Program.
    •        The $20 million Indigenous Primary Health Care Innovation Fund is expanding
             Indigenous virtual care services through the Alberta Indigenous Virtual Care Clinic
             (AIVCC); increasing Indigenous-led cancer screening programs; supporting health
             promotion and disease prevention; along with funding infrastructure and capital
             projects.
To enhance provider capacity, Alberta Health made key investments in team-based care models:

    •        $30 million over two years was allocated to expand team-based care in four community
             health centres: The Alex, CUPS Calgary Society, Jasper Place Wellness Centre, and Radius
             Community Health & Healing.

Recognizing the critical role of family physicians, the Government of Alberta announced a
modernized physician compensation model on December 19, 2024. This model is designed to
attract, retain, and support family physicians and rural generalists. In addition, $13.8 million was
allocated over two-years to PCA to identify new, and expand and scale existing innovative solutions
that provide comprehensive, team-based, primary care services. The NPPCP also saw significant


Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                     41
Results Analysis



uptake. This program supports NPs to operate independently. Since April 2024, 145 applications
have been submitted, with 65 approved. As of March 31, 2025, 53 NPs are practising autonomously
under the NPPCP, with 63,769 medically necessary services provided to 31,905 Albertans. NPPCP
NPs are primarily practising urban communities, with 20 of the 53 NPs in rural communities such
as Beaverlodge, Cold Lake, Two Hills, and Vermilion.

To further support rural emergency care, Alberta launched a pilot project on November 25, 2024,
introducing virtual emergency physicians in five rural emergency departments (EDs): Edson,
Hinton, Lac La Biche, Elk Point, and Beaverlodge. This program enables remote physicians to assess
patients by video, supported by on-site nurses and paramedics. It helps prevent temporary ED
closures, allowing more patients to receive local care. Patients needing in-person emergency care
will still be transferred by EHS to a nearby hospital.

The performance indicator 2.a reflects the number of registered family physicians and NPs in
Alberta. Having an adequate supply of physicians and nurse practitioners would ensure that
Alberta's health care system is always responsive to Albertans' health needs. The number of
registered family physicians and nurse practitioners reflects the system’s capacity to provide
timely, accessible, and community-based care across Alberta.

The number of registered family physicians in Alberta continues to grow annually. More family
physicians and NPs are now registered to practice in Alberta compared with previous years. As of
December 31, 2024, there were 5,951 family physicians and 1,017 NPs registered in Alberta, which
was a net gain of 295 physicians (5.2 per cent increase) and 130 NPs (14.7 per cent increase),
respectively, compared with the same period last year. Expanding the family physician and
registered nurse workforce will improve the efficiency and quality of primary health care delivery
across the province.

2.5    Strengthen the health care system for Albertans and health care providers through the
       use and integration of information technology

The Government of Alberta is actively improving the digital health environment to provide
Albertans with access to their health information and to give health care providers more complete
digital patient information at the point of care. These efforts enhance care quality and support
informed decision-making. Considerable investments are being made to modernize Alberta’s digital
health infrastructure.




42                                                                                           Health | Annual Report 2024–2025
                                                                                        Results Analysis



As of November 2024, Connect Care was fully implemented across AHS and affiliated facilities. It
currently supports 125,000 staff, physicians, and clinicians across AHS, affiliated facilities, and
provincial health agencies that currently operate. Significant progress was also made in advancing
the following digital tools for both patients and providers:

    •        As of March 31, 2025, MyHealth Records (MHR), a portal that allows Albertans to access
             their health information, surpassed 1.9 million registrants.
    •        Over 2,100 community clinics and 1,700 pharmacies were connected to Alberta Netcare,
             Alberta’s Electronic Health Record system, improving provider access to patient
             information.
    •        Alberta Netcare enhancements included the Patient Quick View dashboard, Patient
             Summary Report, and docking capabilities, improving usability for more than 45,000
             clinicians.
    •        The Community Information Integration (CII) and Central Patient Attachment Registry
             (CPAR) continued to expand. Over 2.7 million patients were paneled in CPAR and over
             28.6 million Clinical Encounter Digests were shared through CII.
    •        The Electronic Referral Solution completed several key software upgrades in Phase 2.
             The system is progressing through a closed-loop provincial eReferral model, which
             remains the objective in the next reporting period.
    •        The ministry completed stakeholder consultations and developed a roadmap for an
             Electronic Medical Records (EMR) Certification Program. The initial $500,000 phase
             was completed on December 15, 2024. This program will strengthen system
             interoperability and patient data integration across Alberta.

The ministry completed a proof of concept of AI-driven automated rules extraction and analysis. A
detailed roadmap was developed for modernizing the eligibility and premiums (EAP) legacy
system. Future modernization work includes implementing the EAP roadmap and further
replacement of legacy mainframe systems used in the Canadian public health benefits management
and claims processing domain.

Data integration through the provincial Health Information Exchange was strengthened. New
clinical data sources were added to Alberta Netcare, including diagnostic imaging and reports,
cardiology, pulmonary function tests, laboratory results, community encounter records, and clinic
consultation reports. These additions support more comprehensive and coordinated patient care.

In 2024–25, the Government of Alberta initiated a phased implementation of Lab eOrdering. This
will introduce a closed-loop electronic ordering solution to enhance the efficiency and accuracy of
laboratory test requisitions. The first phase developed a minimum viable product, creating a
foundation for a fully integrated electronic laboratory ordering system.

An independent review of Alberta’s IT systems was completed in summer 2024. A new Digital
Health Operating Model that aligns with a refocused health system is currently in development
based on findings from the independent review.

Alberta Health is also in the process of standing up a Shared Services Entity (SSE) that consolidates
Alberta's digital health IT and supports the new Digital Health Operating Model. The SSE
consolidates digital health IT resourcing to provide services across the new Provincial Health
Agencies and Health Service Delivery Organizations of the Refocused Health System. The SSE is
slated to become operational in September 2025.

Alberta Health, collaboratively with PCA, is working on an initiative to enhance Alberta Netcare
with access to Connect Care for mixed context community providers. A targeted rollout to a select



Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                      43
Results Analysis



group of community providers (determined in partnership with the AMA) is underway and
scheduled to complete by fall 2025.

2.6   Implement strategies that ensure the sustainability of publicly funded pharmaceutical
      benefits

The Government of Alberta remains committed to addressing gaps in drug coverage while ensuring
that drug benefits programs are sustainable and meet the needs of Albertans. Prescription drugs
help manage illnesses, improve quality of life, and, in some cases, preclude the need for more
extensive treatments. The province continued to focus on ensuring value for money spent on these
benefits through improving existing drug benefit programs and adding innovative, effective
therapies.

In December 2024, Alberta signed a bilateral agreement with the federal government to receive
approximately $54 million annually over three years. This funding supports access to new and
emerging drugs for rare diseases and expands screening and diagnostics, improving health
outcomes for Albertans. This funding will provide Albertans with earlier access to a common set of
rare disease drugs, and additional funding for rare disease drugs that are currently offered in
Alberta.

The first new drugs funded under the agreement include Poteligeo for Sézary syndrome, Oxlumo
for hyperoxaluria type 1, and Epkinly for large B-cell lymphoma treatments. It is expected that
approximately 30 Albertans will be eligible annually for these new treatments. Additional drugs
added include Koselugo for neurofibromatosis type 1, Yescarta for follicular lymphoma, and
Welireg for Von Hippel-Lindau disease.

The Government of Alberta also reviewed the federal government’s An Act Respecting Pharmacare
(National Pharmacare Act), which received royal assent on October 10, 2024. The Minister of Health
notified the federal government that Alberta will monitor developments and outcomes of the
federal government’s discussions with other provinces and territories on the National Pharmacare
Act. Alberta requested their fair share of federal funding - without conditions - to strengthen its
existing pharmacare programs and expand coverage.



44                                                                      Health | Annual Report 2024–2025
                                                                                       Results Analysis



In 2024, Alberta Health worked with the Alberta Pharmacists’ Association (RxA) and identified that
pharmacy expenditures were projected to exceed the 2024-25 budgeted threshold. To address this,
cost‑saving measures were introduced on November 1, 2024:

    •        The initial assessment fee for a Comprehensive Annual Care Plan (CACP) was reduced to
             $70 from $100.
    •        The maximum allowed annual follow-up visits for the CACP and Standard Medication
             Management Assessment care plans were reduced from 12 to 4.


These changes align with practices in other jurisdictions and actual service use in Alberta. While
pharmacy fees for some clinical pharmacy services, specifically care plans offered by pharmacists to
Albertans, were adjusted, Albertans continue to have access to assessments and necessary follow-
ups. Core pharmacy services – including dispensing, injections, prescribing, and prescription
renewals - were not affected.

The policy changes on November 1, 2024, have resulted in approximately $19 million in cost-offsets
for 2024-25. Although fee changes provided some financial relief, ongoing expenditures are still
exceeding budget projections due to additional pressures such as increased demand for pharmacy
services and population growth. Alberta Health will continue consultations with the RxA into 2025,
aiming to identify further sustainability measures and develop a new pharmacy framework for
2026 onwards.

The Government of Alberta continued its efforts to reduce prescription drug costs and expanded
access to life-saving drugs. On November 5, 2024, Alberta expanded access to Trikafta to include
individuals with rare mutations of cystic fibrosis who could benefit from treatment. An estimated
70 additional patients will now be able to access this therapy.

In January 2024, COVID-19 vaccine fees were reduced from $25 per dose to $17 per dose. This was
further reduced to $13 per dose on April 1, 2024. This aligns with the fee for administration of
other publicly funded vaccines. The fee for COVID assessment was removed in October 2023.

Access to additional vaccines was expanded. The publicly funded list now includes tetanus,
diphtheria and acellular pertussis (TDaP) and pneumococcal and respiratory syncytial virus (RSV)
vaccines. To support outreach programs, the administration fee for pharmacists delivering vaccines
to vulnerable populations in outreach facilities was increased from $13 to $17 as part of the Alberta
Immunization Outreach Program.

Alberta also advanced its biosimilars initiative with the addition of two categories: Ustekinumab
and Denosumab. These additions are expected to save $10 million annually. Alberta also benefited
from national negotiations through the pan-Canadian Pharmaceutical Alliance (pCPA). In 2023-24,
public drug programs saved an estimated $4.63 billion, with cumulative savings over $24 billion
since inception in 2010. Alberta’s portion of these savings is estimated to be $463 million in 2023-
2024 and $2.4 billion since 2010 based on our portion of public drug spending.

Outcome Three: The health and well-being of all Albertans is protected, supported and
improved, and health inequities among underserved populations, including Indigenous
Peoples, are reduced
The ministry continues to work with its partners to address health inequities for underserved
populations, empower patients, promote health and wellness and give Albertans choice when
receiving care from health care professionals.

Key Objectives
3.1       Safeguard Albertans from communicable diseases, such as food-borne illnesses,
          vaccine preventable diseases (e.g., measles, influenza), respiratory illnesses, and
          sexually transmitted and blood borne infections that can cause severe health
          outcomes through prevention and health promotion measures and policies.

Alberta Health remains committed to safeguarding the health and well-being of Albertans through
proactive measures to prevent the spread of several communicable diseases, such as influenza,
measles, and sexually transmitted and blood borne infections (STBBIs). Immunization programs
remain one of the core public health functions that continues to be a key strategy in preventing
disease and reducing strain on the acute care system.

In 2024-25, Alberta allocated approximately $148 million to support the continued delivery of
provincially funded vaccines, including routine infant, preschool, and school programs. DTaP-IPV-
Hib dose four immunization coverage rates by children aged two decreased from 71 per cent in
2023 to 69 per cent in 2024. Measles, mumps, rubella immunization coverage rates by age two
decreased from 82 per cent in 2023 to 80 per cent in 2024. These immunization rates are both
lower than the national target of 95 per cent for these vaccines (table below).

Childhood immunization rates in Alberta have continued to decline over the last five years, which
led to an increase in vaccine-preventable disease cases and outbreaks (e.g., ongoing pertussis
circulation and newly identified measles cases across the province). To improve these rates, AHS
implemented targeted initiatives to address vaccine hesitancy. For example, AHS is recalling
children for overdue immunizations, monitoring wait times, and developing a provincial
appointment standard to address delays. The AHS Immunization Priority Working Group advanced
its five-year workplan, including the development of:

      •      Provincial immunization orientation modules, including a vaccine hesitancy module to
             assist immunizers in conversations with vaccine hesitant individuals

      •      Immunization resources in multiple languages

                                                                                                 Results Analysis

This performance measure reports the percentage of children in Alberta who, by two years of age,
have received the required immunization for the following vaccine preventable diseases:
diphtheria, tetanus, pertussis (whooping cough), Haemophilus influenza type b, measles, mumps
and rubella. A high rate of immunization reduces the incidence of these diseases and serves to
control outbreaks.

Measles cases have surged globally since 2024, partly due to reduced vaccinations during the
COVID-19 pandemic. In response to outbreaks, including one in Canada, Alberta Health is updating
guidelines and developing a response plan with partners, including AHS, Indigenous Services
Canada, and Public Health Agency of Canada (PHAC), including PHAC’s measles case and contact
management guidance document. Two cases of travel-acquired measles were reported in Alberta in
2024. As of March 31, 2025, 23 confirmed measles cases were reported, with most of the confirmed
cases being in unimmunized individuals. Measles activity is across multiple AHS zones, with two
cases linked to a multi-jurisdictional outbreak. The source cases in each zone were primarily travel-
acquired.

A pertussis outbreak was declared in January 2023 in the South Zone and outbreaks were
subsequently reported across all zones in the province. A Provincial Pertussis Coordinating
Committee (PPCC) was convened in August 2023 to May 2024 to respond to the increase in
reported pertussis cases to limit further spread. The PPCC included representatives from Alberta
Health, AHS, Indigenous Services Canada, and the Alberta Provincial Laboratory. The last associated
zone pertussis outbreak was closed in July 2024.

The provincial incidence rate for pertussis increased from 0.3 (2022) to 19.1 (2023) per 100,000
population, then declined to 12.3 (2024) per 100,000 population. Between January 1, 2023, and
March 31, 2025, a total of 1,608 cases were reported across all zones, and most cases (52.1 per
cent) involved under-immunized or unimmunized children under age 10. During this time, one
pertussis-related death was reported in 2024.

On November 27, 2024, Alberta Health released its One Health Antimicrobial Resistance (AMR)
Framework for Action, backed by $5 million in funding and a public web page to promote
stakeholder engagement. Alberta Health is now implementing the AMR Framework using a phased
approach in collaboration with Agriculture and Irrigation and Environment and Protected Areas.
Funding will support antimicrobial stewardship in primary, acute, and veterinary care.

The Edmonton Zone shigella outbreak was declared over in March 2025. Alberta Health continued
to participate on the Shigella Outbreak Task Force, alongside AHS, shelters, inner-city agencies, the



Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                               47
Results Analysis



City of Edmonton, local family physicians, and APL, to coordinate resources and discuss options to
limit the spread of shigella. Since the start of the outbreak, there were 448 cases identified, with
309 hospitalizations.

The PHAC, in collaboration with provincial and territorial partners, has incorporated culture-
independent diagnostic tests (CIDTs) into case definitions for some enteric diseases as part of
routine surveillance and reporting. CIDTs rapidly detect organisms such as shigellosis and Shiga
toxin-producing E. coli for timely case and contact management. Alberta Health and APL helped
develop these updated national case definitions that were published by the Government of Canada
in January 2024. The work is being continued with the federal government to review and
implement the updated case definitions, while also updating the provincial enteric case definitions
and disease management guidelines.

Alberta has experienced a rapid increase in rates of STBBIs over the last decade like several
jurisdictions in Canada and around the world. In 2022, rates reached an all-time high for the
current syphilis outbreak in Alberta, where 73.5 infectious syphilis cases (per 100,000 population)
and 174.4 congenital syphilis cases (per 100,000 live births) were reported. Since then, rates have
been decreasing but remain high overall.

In 2024, there were 43.9 infectious syphilis cases (per 100,00 population) and 98.4 congenital
syphilis cases (per 100,000 live births) reported. The goal in Alberta is to have zero congenital
syphilis cases. Despite these decreases, Alberta’s current rates of human immunodeficiency virus
(HIV), chlamydia, and gonorrhea have increased by 66.2 per cent, 0.8 per cent, and 4.7 per cent
respectively compared with 2022, highlighting the continued need for public health vigilance and
intervention.

The decrease in infectious syphilis rates reflects a multi-pronged approach to effectively address
the outbreak including:

     •        Syphilis point-of-care testing
     •        Dedicated resources for partner notification, contact tracing, and outreach teams
     •        Targeted education, testing and treatment programs

Despite an improvement from the previous year, the rates of syphilis and congenital syphilis are
still too high and continue to represent a significant public health concern for Alberta.

AHS continues to work on initiatives to increase syphilis awareness, which include a provincial

The Alberta Outreach Program started the week of September 30, 2024, to immunize those at
highest risk of severe outcomes from influenza and COVID-19. The program focuses on providing
immunization services to those at highest risk of severe outcomes, hospitalization, and death due to
vaccine preventable diseases, such as, influenza and COVID-19, as well as invasive pneumococcal
disease, and RSV.

The 2024-25 Influenza and COVID-19 Immunization Programs for the general public began on

      •      13.7 per cent had received a COVID-19 vaccine

3.2       Modernize and strengthen public health legislation to better respond to future public
          health emergencies.

The Government of Alberta remains committed to supporting all Albertans by continuing to
respond promptly, flexibly, and collaboratively to future public health emergencies across all areas
of government. In 2024-25, Alberta Health has focused on strengthening the Alberta workforce and
relationships with the AMA to achieve this goal.

Alberta Health provided $24 million to the AMA to administer a Panel Management Support
Program (PMSP) to help offset costs of maintaining and growing a panel of patients. The PMSP is a
three-year initiative designed to increase access for Albertans to insured medical services.

Alberta Health provided $92 million to the AMA to administer a Transitional Funding Program to
provide physicians with financial support for the cost of eligible expenses related to panel



Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                     49
Results Analysis



management and practice improvement activities. This grant funding was designed to increase
access for Albertans to insured medical services.

Alberta Health collaborated with the AMA to develop a new PCPCM to compensate Alberta’s family
medicine and rural generalist physicians. The PCPCM was implemented on April 1, 2025, and
reaffirms Alberta’s family doctors as among the best compensated and most patient‑focused in the
country. The model is structured to:

      •      Encourage physicians to grow the number of patients they care for
      •      Promote encouragement of full‑time practice
      •      Provide incentives to physicians maintaining high panel numbers, after‑hours care, use
             of improved technology to enhance patient care, and team‑based care

These efforts aim to strengthen Alberta’s primary care system and help reduce pressure on other
parts of the health care system.

3.3       Improve access for underserved populations including First Nations, Métis, and Inuit
          Peoples to high-quality and culturally safe health care services that support
          improved health outcomes.

The Government of Alberta continues to prioritize the health care needs of First Nations, Métis, and
Inuit peoples residing in Alberta. Alberta Health works with health and community partners
including First Nations and Métis leaders and the Government of Canada to ensure that health
services are accessible and culturally appropriate. This commitment entails working with
Indigenous leaders, the Government of Canada, and other partners in a way that respects the
unique and complex needs of these communities. Part of this commitment includes several
initiatives underway to improve health outcomes.

The Indigenous Primary Health Care Implementation Panel developed implementation strategies,
including an implementation road map for the 22 recommendations outlined in the March 31, 2023,
final report to the former Minister of Health on improving the primary health care system for First
Nations, Métis, and Inuit patients in Alberta. On April 19, 2024, The Way Forward document was
publicly released, including the panel’s implementation roadmap, which outlines the sequencing
and timelines for implementing the recommendations. To date, Indigenous MAPS continues to
make progress on the recommendations. The Way Forward is a living document to be updated over
time as implementation moves forward and the provincial health system evolves.

Although the Indigenous Primary Health Care Implementation Panel’s mandate formally concluded
on December 31, 2024, The Way Forward document continues to ensure a more inclusive, culturally
safe health care system for First Nations, Métis, and Inuit communities in Alberta. Ongoing updates
to the document will help improve health outcomes with the design of new health care policies and
programs informed by direct input from these communities, leading to more culturally appropriate,
accessible, and effective care.

Administered by Alberta Health under the Indigenous MAPS stream, the Indigenous Primary Health
Care Innovation Fund (Innovation Fund) aims to enhance access to culturally safe and accessible
primary health care for First Nations, Métis, and Inuit peoples. The Innovation Fund improves
health outcomes by fostering better engagement, increased adherence to treatment, reduced health
disparities, and ensuring that care respects cultural needs and knowledge.

The Innovation Fund offers two funding streams: Tier One provides up to $2 million for community
capacity-building and testing promising practices, while Tier Two offers up to $3 million to scale or
expand Indigenous-led programs, initiatives, and infrastructure. In the first round, two grant


50                                                                        Health | Annual Report 2024–2025
                                                                                      Results Analysis



recipients were selected to receive funding: the First Nations Technical Services Advisory Group
Inc., expanding the AIVCC, and the Métis Nation of Alberta, enhancing their Medical Travel Program
to improve health access for Métis peoples. The third and final round of the Innovation Fund closed
on November 15, 2024.

The Innovation Fund was awarded to the First Nations Technical Services Advisory Group Inc. This
grant will support the existing core clinic and mental health teams hire staff and develop resources
to better meet growing demands and expand cancer screening. The fund helps to improve access to
primary and mental health care for First Nations, Métis, and Inuit individuals, especially in remote
or underserved areas. Expanding cancer screening programs and increasing mental health support
will help with early detection, prevention, and better management of health conditions, ultimately
leading to improved overall health for these communities.

The Indigenous Patient Navigator Program provides financial support to recruit and retain
navigators who assist First Nations, Métis, and Inuit patients, communities, and organizations in
accessing primary health care services. The $7.6 million over three years provided by the program
improves care coordination, reduces barriers, and enhances health outcomes by ensuring
personalized support, timely access to health care services, and better management of diseases or
health conditions. Following the success of this program, the Government of Canada contributed an
additional $4 million to launch a second call of navigator program funding through the Bilateral
Indigenous Patient Navigator Grant Program.

Alberta Health approved the Indigenous Anti-Racism in Health Care Strategy to address perceived
racism in Alberta’s health care system. To help shape the strategy, the Indigenous Health Division
hosted 28 virtual sessions and 11 in-person sessions, in addition to an online feedback form in fall
2024. The sessions aimed to hear the lived experiences and perspectives of First Nations, Métis, and
Inuit peoples, and health system partners. The anti-racism information gathering sessions fostered
collaboration between these communities, health care providers, and key organizations to create a
more culturally sensitive and inclusive health care system. The feedback gathered will help shape
the Anti-Racism in Health Care Strategy, ensuring it addresses the specific needs and concerns of
communities, ultimately improving health care delivery and trust. The anti-racism information
gathering sessions will help contribute to improved health outcomes by ensuring that the action
plan is tailored to the unique needs of these communities, promoting a health care system that is
more culturally sensitive and responsive. The ministry continues to uphold its commitment to
Jordan’s Principle by ensuring First Nations children receive timely access to publicly funded
health, education, and social services without denials, delays, or disruptions due to jurisdictional
issues tied to their First Nations status.

In early January 2025, the Minister of Health met with Health Canada, ISC, and representatives from
various Indigenous communities in Edmonton, including the Métis Nation of Alberta, Metis
Settlements General Council, Treaty 6 and Treaty 8 First Nations, the Blackfoot Confederacy, and
the Stoney Nakoda Tsuut’ina Tribal Council. These meetings are crucial for addressing key
priorities and concerns of communities. The discussions aimed to improve access to essential
health services and ensure that policies better meet the unique needs of First Nation, Métis, and
Inuit communities.

3.4      Prevent injuries and manage chronic diseases and conditions through policy
         development, health and wellness promotion, improved access to screening, and
         initiatives that facilitate wellbeing in healthy environments.

The Government of Alberta is leading policies and initiatives to improve the well-being of
Albertans. In 2024-25, this included areas such as maternal, infant and early childhood
development; injury prevention; and the advancement of population wellness and health equity.


Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                   51
Results Analysis



Alberta Health and AHS continue to prioritize access to reproductive health and maternity services
for Indigenous, vulnerable, and rural populations. Budget 2024 provided over $2 million in annual
funding to help vulnerable mothers and their babies access prenatal and postnatal care and social
supports. Alberta Health has also committed $20 million in funding to the Alberta Women’s Health
Foundation and the Calgary Health Foundation to support research on women’s health matters. The
funding for both foundations will support the implementation and evaluation of clinical programs
including a rapid access clinic as well as support research of women’s health conditions.

In 2024-25, the government focused on ensuring safe and reliable drinking water for Albertans by
working with national groups and Environment and Protected Areas to implement the Canadian
Drinking Water guidelines. The Alberta government also supported and funded a provincial
educational program and provided water testing for well water users, helping them manage their
water resources safely and effectively.

Under the Alberta Safe Beach protocol, a prioritized list of recreational water sites were analyzed
for water quality in support of healthy environments and prevention of waterborne illness. This
contributes to the Ministry's objective to promote health and wellness and prevent chronic disease
through initiatives that facilitate wellbeing in safe environments. An estimated 488 cyanobacterial
bloom toxicity (microcytin) tests, 2,403 Enterococcus (fecal indicator) tests, and 1,070 speciation of
fecal source (i.e. human vs ruminant) tests were completed. Informed by these results, 23 fecal
contamination and 38 cyanobacteries advisories were issued to safeguard public health and
prevent waterborne illnesses. A Waterborne Pathogen Monitoring Grant was used to investigate
new recreational water testing methods and has concluded as of March 31, 2025.

The Lifesaving Society's Alberta Safe Beach Drowning Reduction Project targeted waterfront
operators and the public, focusing on standardized water safety signage, public education, and
promoting safe aquatic environments. The project focused on 15 provincially identified high-risk
locations to enhance aquatic safety for Albertans. The project has concluded and came in
underbudget with an approximate total cost of $25,300 (against approximately $28,200).

Alberta Health facilitated the management of chronic diseases and promotion of health and
wellness through the administration of grants that contribute to building healthier communities
across the province. These grants supported community-based programs focused on healthy eating,
active living, physical literacy, recreational programming, mental wellness, and social connection
while also empowering health care providers to prescribe physical activity. Alberta Health provided
$960,000 in funding to promote physical activity, healthy eating, and active living for the
management of chronic disease and promotion of health and wellness. It also provided the Injury
Prevention Centre $800,000 per year for three years through programs and campaigns that reduce
the burden of injuries on individuals and the health care system, enhancing the system’s capacity to
respond to other needs.

Government continues to support cancer screening programs to return screening rates to pre-
pandemic levels. Screening programs continue to be supported, such as the Alberta Lung Cancer
screening pilot project, the Colorectal Cancer Screening Program, and the Breast Cancer Screening
program, which support the early detection and treatment of cancer. The Alberta Newborn
Screening Program continues to be supported. The program screens infants for conditions that can
be treated early, when treatment can benefit the most, with the primary goal of reducing morbidity
and mortality among infants born in Alberta.

In response to the September 4, 2023, E. coli outbreak in 11 licensed childcare facilities in Calgary,
the Government of Alberta appointed the Food Safety and Licensed Facility-Based Child Care
Review Panel. The panel identified opportunities for strengthening the legislation and inspection
processes that govern food safety in kitchens for licensed childcare facilities. In June 2024, the


52                                                                          Health | Annual Report 2024–2025
                                                                                     Results Analysis



review panel submitted its final report of 12 main recommendations and 27 sub-recommendations.
Implementation of the review panel’s recommendations began in July 2024. The impacts of this
work include extensive review and anticipated changes to ensure food safety is practiced and
enforced in licensed childcare facilities in our province. This will ensure that children and
vulnerable Albertans will experience improved safety when they are served food. Although the
review panel started its review in November 2023, the expenditures on this initiative were applied
in the 2024-2025 budget. Close to $144,000 paid for activities related to the review panel’s
operations, including about $59,000 in honoraria, $3,200 in travel expenses, $50,000 in consulting
fees to conduct engagement with families, and about $21,600 for an independent investigation on
the response and outcomes of the outbreak.

The Government of Alberta is committed to improving access to medical technology that helps
Albertans better manage their health. On December 16, 2024, Alberta Health expanded access to
continuous glucose monitors to Albertans over the age of 18 enrolled in an Alberta government
sponsored supplementary benefit program, who are living with diabetes and require certain insulin
therapies. This fulfils a key recommendation from the Diabetes Working Group, which provided
advice to government on improving Alberta’s diabetes care pathway. Approximately 23,500
Albertans aged 18 years and older will now be eligible for continuous glucose monitor coverage.
Funding for the first two years of this initiative is provided through the Canada-Alberta agreement
to improve health care.

Performance Measure and Indicator Methodology
Performance Measure 1.a:
     Emergency department wait times: 90th percentile time to initial physician assessment
     in the 16 largest sites (hours)

Methodology

This measure represents the length of time (in hours) between patient triage and the time of the
initial physician assessment.

The 90th percentile response interval is calculated for all EMS events triaged by the EMS
Communications Centre as ‘delta’ or ‘echo’, the most emergent or potentially life-threatening calls.

Source

AHS – Emergency Department Information Systems and Emergency Department Visit Data
Repository AHS.

Performance Measure 1.b:
EMS 90th percentile response times (minutes) for the most urgent (life threatening) calls

Methodology

Response time is calculated based on events triaged in the EMS Communication Centre, using an
internationally accredited EMS call taking system, to be emergency or potentially life threatening at
the time of triage by the EMS Communications Centre. This includes events categorized as ‘delta’ and
‘echo.’

     •       Inclusion: Event level response interval for all requests for EMS response triaged as a
             ‘delta’ or ‘echo’ by geographical location of call (Metro/Urban, Communities > 3,000,
             Rural or Remote).

     •       Exclusions:

             o     Events triaged by the EMS Communications Centre as an alpha, bravo or charlie
                   response.
             o     Events or requests identified as inter-facility transfers.
             o     Events with missing timestamps (such as arrive on scene) related to metric
                   calculation.
The 90th percentile response interval is calculated for all EMS events triaged by the EMS
Communications Centre as ‘delta’ or ‘echo’, the most emergent or potentially life-threatening calls.

Source

EMS Computer Aided Dispatch (CAD) data captured with EMS Communications Centres, Alberta
Health




54                                                                              Health | Annual Report 2024–2025
                                                                                        Results Analysis



Performance Indicator 2.a:
     Number of practising family physicians and registered nurse practitioners in Alberta

Methodology

Family physician counts are as of December 31 of each year as reported in the Q4 Quarterly Updates
by the College of Physicians and Surgeons of Alberta (CPSA). CPSA’s year end is December 31, and it
reports the number of physicians every quarter – as of March 31, June 30, September 30, and
December 31.

Note that physician registration counts are cyclical throughout the year. Counts decrease in the first
quarter of the calendar year between January – March, as physicians who do not renew their
registration as of December of the previous year are removed. Numbers increase throughout the
year, particularly in the third quarter between July – September when the majority of new physicians
register after completing residency programs. To reflect the count as at fiscal year end, the registered
family physician numbers have been updated using CPSA data as of March 31, 2025.

Source

College of Physicians and Surgeons of Alberta – https://cpsa.ca/about-cpsa/statistics/



Performance Indicator 2.c:
     Number of Albertans registered for MyHealth Records

Methodology

Number of Albertans who have registered for a MyHealth Records Accounts at the end of each day at
2359EST.

Source

Alberta Health



Performance Indicator 3.a:
     Rate of infectious syphilis per 100,000 population and the rate of congenital syphilis
     per 100,000 live births in Alberta

Methodology

The infectious syphilis rates are calculated by dividing the number of cases (i.e. numerator) by the
number of persons (i.e. denominator). All rates are based on Alberta mid-year population for the
appropriate year and reported per 100,000 population.

The numerator is the number of congenital syphilis cases, derived from the Communicable Disease
& Outbreak Management (CDOM) database for each calendar year. Confirmed and probable cases
are grouped together. Early congenital syphilis cases and syphilitic stillbirths are provided whilst



Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                     55
Results Analysis



late congenital syphilis cases are excluded. STI information, with links to case definitions, are
available online: https://www.alberta.ca/notifiable-diseaseguidelines. Non-Alberta residents are
not included in the numerator. Whenever possible, disease diagnosis dates were used to determine
the year of infection. If the diagnosis date was not available, then the collection date was used. The
denominator is the number of live births in Alberta. The congenital syphilis rate is reported as the
number of congenital syphilis cases diagnosed in Alberta per 100,000 live births.

Source

Communicable Disease & Outbreak Management (CDOM) - Communicable Disease Reporting
System (CDRS), AHCIP Adjusted Mid-Year Population Registry Files, Alberta Health Postal Code
Translator File (PCTF), and Vital Statistics Live Births



Additional Performance Metrics not included in Health Business Plan 2024-27

Performance Indicator (Outcome 1, Key Objective 1.1):
     Percentage of surgical procedures that met national wait time benchmarks

Methodology

Ready-to-treat (RTT) wait time (in days) is calculated for each relevant record meeting specified
inclusion/exclusion criteria.

     •        Inclusion Criteria: All elective hip, knee replacement and cataract surgeries based on
              surgery procedure catalogue descriptions. All urgency levels.
     •        Exclusion Criteria: persons who received emergency surgical care. Cases with an invalid
              RTT date. When cataract surgery is required for both of a patient’s eyes, only the wait
              time for surgery on the first eye is included in the wait time calculations.

RTT Date = The date that the surgeon determines the patient is ready for the surgical intervention.
The RTT Date excludes patient delays or voluntary waits.

Treatment Date = The date the treatment (surgical procedure) took place.

RTT Wait time = Treatment (surgical procedure) Date minus RTT Date

National RTT wait time benchmarks:

     •        Hip replacement national benchmark is 182 days
     •        Knee replacement national benchmark is 182 days
     •        Cataract surgery national benchmark (for first eye) is 112 days.

             Number of cases completed with a RTT wait time less than or equal to the
Percentage = national RTT wait time benchmark                                                     X 100

                   Number of completed cases with valid RTT dates

                                                                                        Results Analysis



Source

Local Operating Room (OR) Information Systems, AHS, and OR Data Repository, AHS

Methodology

Main operating room activity is used as representative of “surgeries” in the province. Based on
identified main operating cases, those which are non-emergency are identified and the percentage
where the site of delivery was a CSF is reported.

     •        Numerator: Non-Emergency MAIN OR cases completed in a CSF.
     •        Denominator: Non-Emergency MAIN OR cases.

                      Mar 31

Performance Indicator (Outcome 1, Key Objective 1.1):
     Percentage of patients seen within established targets for CT scan and MRI scan

Methodology

Data reflects performance as of the last day of the fiscal year (March 31, 2025).

Target Met Percentage includes all patient types: outpatients, emergency, and inpatients.

Target: Refers to the maximum allowable wait time (in days or hours) for an imaging exam, as
defined by provincial benchmarks. Targets vary by imaging modality, clinical priority, and patient
category.

Source

Path to Care DI Dashboard - OWL Production (1.0): Path to Care DI Access Owl - AHS Tableau Server



Performance Indicator (Outcome 2, Key Objective 2.3):
     Unplanned medical readmissions to hospital within 30 days of discharge

Methodology

Numerator: Total number of medical patients with unplanned readmission to hospital within 30
days of discharge.

     •        Inclusion Criteria: Residents of Alberta covered by the Alberta Health Care Insurance
              Plan. Admission day of subsequent readmission is less than or equal to 30 days of initial
              discharge date from an acute care hospital.
     •        Exclusion Criteria: Transfers (admitted within 6 hours of discharge from another
              hospital, or 6-12 hours after transfer from or to an acute care facility); newborn, still
              birth, cadaver admissions; non-acute care admissions; pediatric (less than 20 years of
              age), surgery, obstetrics, palliative care, mental health admissions; hospital admissions
              for cancer therapy; non-urgent, planned admissions.
Denominator: Total number of medical patients discharged.

     •        Inclusion Criteria: Residents of Alberta covered by the Alberta Health Care Insurance
              Plan; discharge from an acute care hospital, within a reporting period.
     •        Exclusion Criteria: Admissions where patients died in hospital; admissions from March 2
              to March 31; transfers (admitted within 6 hours of discharge from another hospital, or 6-
              12 hours after transfer from or to an acute care facility); newborn, still birth, cadaver
              admissions; non-acute care admissions; pediatric (less than 20 years of age), surgery,
              obstetrics, palliative care, mental health admission.

                                                                                          Results Analysis




Hospital                    Number of hospital inpatient discharges where a patient was
Readmission                 readmitted to hospital within 30 days of index discharge
                                                                                          X 100
Percentage   =
(unadjusted)                Number of hospital inpatients discharged

Risk Adjustment: Accounts for differences in patient characteristics that may vary over years. Based
on the list of risk factors (e.g., age, sex, case mixing grouping, Charlson comorbidity score) published
by the Canadian Institute for Health Information (CIHI), and the Alberta-built, provincial specific
logistic regression model using data from the past five years to estimate the Alberta average and
expected readmissions based on patients’ risk profile. The risk-adjusted rate is calculated using the
following formula:

                                        Observed cases
Risk-adjusted rate =                                                   X Alberta rate
                                        Expected cases

The methods used by Alberta and CIHI differ only in that Alberta applies the Alberta Rate instead of
the Canada Rate. Using the Alberta rate allows for timely reporting and calculation of results for sub-
populations. The limitation of this method is that the estimates produced in Alberta are not
comparable with other provinces produced by CIHI. Interprovincial comparisons are still possible
by using the CIHI estimates only (i.e., using the CIHI produced estimate for Alberta, and not the
Alberta produced estimate).

Source

Alberta’s Morbidity and Ambulatory Care Abstract Reporting System (MACAR)

Note: Data in MACAR is then provisioned to the ministry’s Business Intelligence warehouse. It is also
submitted to CIHI’s Discharge Abstract Database (DAD) and National Ambulatory Care Reporting
System (NACRS) datasets.



Performance Indicator (Outcome 3, Key Objective 3.1):
     Children by age two immunized for diphtheria, tetanus, pertussis (whooping cough),
     polio, Haemophilus influenza type b) (DTaP-IPV-Hib) and for measles, mumps, rubella

Methodology

The AHCIP Quarterly Population Registries (AHCIP) provides the basis for the cohorts. Individuals
who are residents of Alberta (including First Nations people) are followed until the earliest of (i)
immunized (ii) leaving Alberta, (iii) death, or (iv) end-of-study period. Immunization records from
Imm/ARI are linked to this cohort using Unique Lifetime Identifier. Residents of Lloydminster are
removed from the analysis, since those residents are captured as part of Saskatchewan Health.




Health | Annual Report 2024–2025                                                                       59
Results Analysis



The coverage rates are based on a birth cohort and reported in percentage by ages of two. To be
considered immunized, a child must have received their age-appropriate dose(s) by their birthday
(e.g., if a child received a dose after their 2nd birthday, they are not counted as having received a
dose by age two). In terms of birth cohort, the 2024 coverage rates relate to the 2022 birth cohort
(i.e. those who were born in 2022) that turned age two in 2024.

Calculation: Childhood immunization coverage is calculated using a survival analysis (time-to-
immunization) method based on the specified population-based birth cohort. The analysis measures
the probability that the child will receive required vaccines by age two.

Source

AHCIP Quarterly Population Registries and Immunization/Adverse Reactions to Immunization
(Imm/ARI).
